good-bye the following day.

And when Lettice felt the clasp of her arms about her neck, as Eudora
kissed her and poured out her grateful thanks, it was a more precious
reward than any other payment.


Ermyn was in a wild state of delight when she looked out and saw her
mother coming across the meadows. Lettice had not told her for certain
that she would return, lest anything might detain her. It was indeed a
festive homecoming, for kind neighbours gathered round to tell her how
glad they were to see her back again.

And Ermyn, proudly and shyly, drew from the cupboard a cake of her own
making and baking, which was duly partaken of and praised.

"And, mother," cried the child, with a shout of delight; "Dick Rees has
just brought this letter, and I am sure it must be from David! Come
close to granddad, and read it now. Please do, there's a good mother!"

David's glad mother needed no second bidding, and the letter was
accordingly read over and over again; for it contained the joyful news
that her son would arrive the last week in June to spend his six weeks'
holiday at home in the dear old cottage. It had been a long time since
he had allowed himself to have this treat, for the journey was an
expensive one, and he had thought it his duty, in these leisure times,
to accept invitations to assist ministers in the Connexion who needed
help, being thus enabled to save up a nice little sum towards his next
homecoming.

He had done well and honourably in his college life, and his tutors
spoke in the highest terms of his conduct. His mind had developed
freely under the great advantages and careful training he had received.
He felt that God had called him to the high and noble work of the
ministry, and looked forward with intense pleasure to the fulfilment of
his hopes.

St. Elvis felt not a little proud of his success, after his education
at the town grammar school, and intended to give him a hearty welcome
when he should appear in his native place again. But when one day
the crazy old coach stopped at "The Jolly Sailors," and amongst the
passengers a tall gentlemanly young man in black stepped down, no one
recognised him.

Lettice and Ermyn had been watching together for him at the inn door,
and thought he had not come, when suddenly the good woman became aware
that the stranger who had run towards her and was now giving her a
hearty hug was none other than her own son David.

"And where is my little sister?" he said, drawing the blushing Ermyn
closer to him, and looking with surprise and delight at her lovely
face. "I am still a little taller than she is," he added, laughing, as,
giving an arm to each, the joyful trio set out towards home.

Those were indeed happy holidays, for not a cloud appeared to dim any
one's enjoyment. More homes than one rejoiced together, for there was
gladness also in the doctor's household. Mary had come home at last,
not to say good-bye again; and Mrs. Llewellyn was full of motherly
pride to welcome her handsome son Maurice, now a hard-working medical
student, who hoped some day to return and share his father's practice.

The old Rectory smiled also, for Eudora, though still looking like a
fragile flower, grew strong enough to come out and enjoy the summer
sunshine and fresh sea breezes. A quiet donkey was found for her, and
the good old times seemed to come back again, as David walked beside
her, leading it carefully, while Ermyn skipped and sang for joy. There
was no caste in that simple village town, for those who loved each
other had the strongest ties to bind them together.

Ermyn, the fisher-maiden, still called Eudora her sister-friend, and
Mary loved them both. Maurice hailed his old companion more heartily
than ever, and the two would walk together for miles along the lonely
coast, or climb the serrated ranges of rock to lofty heights, whence
they could view the whole peninsula of Dewisland, that terminates in
the bold headlands and jagged rocks so often fatal to unfortunate
mariners. Their geological and botanical studies were pursued with
interest, and they would sit and discuss their treasures among
Druidical circles and great boulders.

It was such pleasant companionship that neither liked to think how soon
they must part again. Both young men felt, as they trod the streets of
David's native town, that good soldiers of Jesus Christ were sorely
needed there; soldiers brave and strong to fight against the sin and
ignorance around; and they earnestly prayed that this great work might
some day be theirs to do. But they did not need to wait till the
future to begin it. David found leisure during his holiday to read and
pray with the sick and dying, and Maurice gave his help and sympathy
wherever he found it needed. Thus they called down upon themselves the
blessings of the poor.


It sometimes happens that a season of unusual brightness is given us
as a preparation for cloudy days. The cup running over, and the table
ready spread, may be in the presence of unseen enemies and trials,
against which we must be strengthened to go forward and fight. Those
happy summer days came to a close all too quickly, and autumn winds and
falling leaves were heralding the reign of winter, which was expected
to begin early this year.

Eudora seldom went outside the Rectory now, for her severe illness had
left her painfully susceptible to cold. And a short dry cough smote
sadly on the ears of all who heard it. Yet Dora had never been so happy
in her home-life before, for there was nothing in its atmosphere to
chill or distress her now, though her aunt was as full of life and
generous activity as ever. Mrs. Llewellyn and Lettice rejoiced together
to see all that capability and energy, like a glad stream, flowing in
the sunshine, instead of in the shadows.

Silexa had brought her talents to the Master, as the disciples did of
old with the few loaves and fishes, and He had blessed them. Now she
could feed a multitude, and yet have enough and to spare. But her Lord
must test and try her still further. And as the burden pressed more
heavily, so by His grace did she grow stronger to bear it, and face
what to her was a terrible trial. They must leave the Rectory for a
year. The doctor had said that, humanly speaking, this was the only
chance for saving Eudora's life.

Thorough change to a dryer and more sheltered climate might revive the
flickering flame, which seemed gradually but surely dying out. The
sacrifice must be made, and that at once. Silexa would not send away
her child amongst strangers, and she could not leave her father in his
infirm state. There was no time to be lost.

A substitute for the rector was found in a young clergyman with private
means and temporarily without a charge. Mary Llewellyn promised to go
daily to the Rectory to look after Molly and Dinah, give out the parish
stores, and to help in every way, so far as her duties to her mother
would permit.

Some travelling trunks of a very ancient date were routed out from
their repose of years in one of the Rectory garrets, where they had
been well-nigh buried among the lumber stored there. But the packing
was weary work, and a sore puzzle to Miss Silexa, whose little world
of interests had all been shut in by St. Elvis and its belongings for
so many, many years. She rummaged among the old stores, and while she
did so, hidden things were brought to light, which caused a flood of
associations to rush in upon her memory.

"I was so happy in that old dress," she said, half aloud, taking up a
scrap of faded plaid. "I cannot bear to throw it away."

She looked tenderly at it for a moment, debating whether it should
be flung into the heap of rubbish at her feet. But it was silently
restored to the place whence she had taken it. A palette and a box of
colours, all caked and dried, and a bundle of paint-brushes next caught
her eye. She took them up one by one, and kissed them with a face
expressive of the deepest agony.

"Oh, Edward, my darling!" she murmured. "Why did you do it? I shall
never see you now again, on this side the grave. But God has you in His
keeping somewhere," she added, calming herself. "His love is infinite."

The packing was finished in time. And on the last evening, when the
boxes were all corded and sent away to the inn overnight to be ready
for the early start on the coach, Miss Silexa unhung her cotton bonnet
from its peg in the hall, and went softly out alone, as the evening
shadows were lengthening.

It had been blowing a hurricane all day, but the storm had passed, and
there was a bright, evening glow. The sun was setting in stormy gold,
circled by black, and the sky was of a pale, green colour, with flaming
amber and copper clouds rushing across it. The hilltops were still
capped with white mist that lifted and floated, and crept down the
sides of the green valley.

Silexa walked far on into the twilight, and then turned towards home.
The rent clouds appeared white near the moon, whose crescent sickle
seemed to cut a path through the surrounding blackness. The wind rushed
and roared through the Rectory trees, making them sway and toss in an
agony of unrest, while every casement in the old house rattled and
shook, as the clinging ivy was made here and there to loosen its clasp
of the dear old walls it sought to shield.

Silexa heeded not the rude gusts; she was looking at the brightness
above her.

"'They shall see His face,'" she murmured; "that is where the path is
leading."

   "'There shall be no night there; and they need no candle, neither light
of the sun; for the Lord God giveth them light: and they shall reign
for ever and ever.'

   "'O send out Thy light and Thy truth: let them lead me; let them bring
me unto Thy holy hill...'

"This night of sorrow is breaking towards eternal day. And though the
shadows increase, a dawn is coming when they shall flee away for ever.

   "'Return unto thy rest, O my soul; for the Lord hath dealt bountifully
with thee.'"


There was quite a crowd round the dilapidated old coach the following
morning, when the little party from the Rectory set out to walk towards
it. Miss Pryse supported her aged, white-haired father, who leant
heavily on her strong arm. Eudora followed, with Mary and Ermyn on
either side of her, and Lettice behind, carrying wraps and parcels.

Hands were stretched out for hearty shakes of farewell; blessings were
uttered and voices trembled with emotion as countless good-byes were
said.

Silexa gave one long look at her old home as the coach lumbered off,
and felt she had been torn up by the roots.



CHAPTER X

THE GOLDEN SHORE

A FLAKE of snow! How slowly it fell through the hushed air! Feathery
light and soft, it came all silently to greet a little daisy, that
looked upward and received it with a smile. It was the herald of a
winter storm, and Lettice from her cottage door looked up into the
darkened sky to watch "the whitening shower fall broad and wide and
fast, dimming the day."

"I wish the child had not gone to St. Elvis this morning," she said to
Old Roger. "She will be storm-stayed now."

"Never trouble, lass," he replied cheerily; "she is in good safe
quarters up at the grey house, though really me!" he added, stepping
outside. "This 'is' snow, and no mistake! We haven't had such a fall
this side Christmas for many a long year. I'm thankful our poor sick
lambie at the Rectory is taken away to a warmer fold. This cold would
have killed her, but I miss her sweet face."

"Ay, indeed, and so do I," said Lettice, with a sigh, as she watched
her father walk towards the jetty.

She felt unusually depressed that morning, but she could not tell
exactly why. A sense of loneliness and desolation had descended on
her with the departure of the family from the Rectory, for she had
leaned more than she was aware on Miss Silexa's firm strength and sober
judgment. Mrs. Gwynne's disposition was naturally timid and shrinking,
though God had made her calm and patient. And now He had some hard
lessons for her, which she must learn "alone" with Him.


Ermyn had gone up early into town to learn tidings of the travellers,
and with dismay she looked out from Mrs. Llewellyn's windows to see
the storm come sweeping up in white masses through the valley. There
was a bright, cheery letter from Miss Silexa, telling of a quiet, easy
journey to Southsands, where they were established in comfortable
lodgings in a terrace looking out upon the quay and shipping.

   "It seems strange," she wrote, "to be in this large, fashionable
watering-place, where we do not know a soul. Mr. Lyndwood has asked
some friends to call on us; and Eudora is already better for the change
of air. Her appetite is considerably improved, and quite a new world
has opened to her wondering eyes."

Ermyn read the letter again and again, dwelling on Dora's message to
herself and Lettice, and wishing she could be with her.

"If you like to write a note, I will put it into mine," said Mrs.
Llewellyn.

So Ermyn wrote one in her clear, pretty hand, folded it, and laid it on
Mrs. Llewellyn's little table, and went again to look out. The weather
was more hopeless than ever. And seeing there was no prospect at
present of fulfilling her promise to return quickly, she took off her
bonnet, and settled down quietly to her work.

Three days passed before she was able to go home. The snow had
continued to fall so heavily, that the road between St. Elvis and
Porthmynwr was completely blocked; and as for the meadow path through
the valley, there was no indication of such a thing. Deep snowdrifts
had filled up the hollows, and it would have been most perilous to
attempt to find a way through them. And when the snow melted, there
would be a swampy waste of waters, abounding in dangerous bogs and
marshes.

On the fourth day, however, a cart track had been cleared along the
high road. And Ermyn was soon running full speed on it, clapping her
hands together that she might feel her numbed fingers, while her warm
breath floated before her like a wreath of smoke.


Lettice came out on the causeway to meet her. And Ermyn saw at once by
the expression on her face that something was the matter.

"Hush, dearie!" she said, raising a warning finger. "Step softly, and
come in as gently as you can. Poor granddad is very ill; he had a bad
fall on the rocks last night. The fog was so thick, and the snow so
slippery with the thaw that he missed his footing, and fell over the
jetty at low tide. It would not have been half so bad if he had fallen
into the water. But, oh, those sharp, cruel rocks! They have hurt his
poor back and head, and I don't think he will ever be able to stand
again. I thought he was dead when they carried him into the house."

The tears had sprung into Ermyn's eyes as she listened, and she flung
her arms round her mother's neck, sobbing.

"Hush, darling!" whispered Lettice. "Don't let him see you crying. A
kind neighbour is beside him now. It was such a pitch-dark night, I
could not send for the doctor. But this morning I saw him riding across
the hill to that cottage, so Jem Lewis has gone after him. I know he
will come as quickly as he can."

"Oh, mother, mother!" said Ermyn in a low tone, following her over the
threshold. "What shall we do? Dear, dear granddad! I wish I had not
gone away."


The good doctor soon obeyed the summons, and gave, as he always did,
words of cheer with his valuable surgical skill.

But Lettice knew by the kind pressure of his hand and sympathising look
as he passed out, that he thought the case a very, very bad one.

"Keep him as quiet as possible," he said; "and you, my little maid, be
a good girl to your mother."

Old Roger was seldom conscious for weeks, and he would moan
distressingly when moved or touched. Yet he prayed, though
incoherently,—

   "Good Master, Dear Lord! I can bear it if Thou art with me in the fire.
Jesus, Lover of my soul."

He never seemed to lose his hold of Christ, and would repeat fragments
of texts, and a line here and there from hymns that he had learned and
loved during his time of health.

"Where are the lads?" he said one night. "They haven't 'all' come to
Jesus yet; no, not 'all.' But the door of mercy is open wide, and 'I
will in no wise cast out,' says Christ."

Neighbours and fishermen would come crowding in with inquiries and
offers of help. And Lettice feared they must think her very unkind as
she entreated them not to stand in the doorway, keeping the pure air
from the sick man. But she could not prevent one and another stealing
in now and then, on tiptoe, for a look at the dear old patriarch.
And his mates would beg her to let them take it in turn to share the
night-watch with her. This was an offer she gladly accepted, for the
strain was daily growing heavier. There is much real sympathy and true
kindness often to be met with among the poor, and none knew this better
than Lettice during her long, bitter trial.


"You must write to Davie," she said one day to Ermyn, as Christmas drew
near, "and tell him to come home, if possible, for these holidays. I
fear if he is prevented, he will not see dear granddad again."

This was the first intimation of danger Ermyn had comprehended and hot
tears fell on the paper as she wrote. But it did her good to pour her
heart out to David.

He came as quickly as possible, and with his coming, hope lightened
the heavy heart of his stricken mother. His grandfather knew him as
he entered and sat down by the bedside, and the wrinkled hand was
stretched out to take David's within its feeble grasp.

"My boy," he said, looking at him, "thy old granddad is brought very
low, but the Lord is my light and my salvation. I will trust, and not
be afraid."

The cottage was not so dreary after that, for David was the support and
comforter of all, during his month's sojourn at home. It seemed to give
Roger a lift, for he was certainly better, and able to be propped up in
bed, and even got as far as to sit out sometimes in his great chair by
the fire within shelter of the settle.

Mr. Williams, the clergyman in charge, was most kind and unwearied
in his visits. He would come to read and pray beside Roger, and talk
cheerfully to him about what was going on at St. Elvis, encouraging him
in return to tell him some of his sailor yarns to help to pass those
long winter days.

David conceived an enthusiastic admiration for Mr. Williams, and
whenever he could be spared would find his way to the lodgings over
the post-office, where the two young men would enter into long and
interesting discussions on all kinds of subjects, which David found
most refreshing in the midst of his anxiety.

"I find my inability to speak the Welsh tongue such a drawback," Mr.
Williams said to him one day. "I did not study it until I was grown-up,
and although I know it well enough to get through the service pretty
well, and read some sermons Dr. Pryse lent me, I find I cannot get at
the people in their homes, for when they begin to talk it fast, I am
obliged to tell them I cannot understand. I suppose you speak it well?"

"Oh yes," laughed David; "but no merit to me, for I have been
accustomed to it from my cradle. We never speak anything else at home
unless strangers come in. Grandfather likes it best, and I love the
sound of the Welsh tongue."

"How I wish you were in our Church," rejoined the other; "it stands in
great need of Welsh-speaking clergymen."

In reply, David confided to Mr. Williams his final resolution to seek
ordination to the Episcopalian ministry, adding that his fondest wishes
would be attained could he but be found worthy to aid the venerable
pastor of St. Elvis in tending there the flock of Christ.

"But what," said Mr. Williams, "will the friends say by whom your
education has been provided?"

"Some of them will be disappointed, no doubt. But from the first they
left me free to decide on my future course."

"Then you can return to college under these altered circumstances?"

"Assuredly; and should I ever be able to do so, I shall in any case
repay the cost of my education, or provide, in time, for some other
poor student, without asking to what church he belongs."

On this, the friends parted; Mr. Williams, who had considerable
influence, silently resolving to forward David's purpose to the
uttermost.


But the cloud that had opened to show its silver lining while David
remained at home closed over again dark and drear soon after his
departure. There was no prospect of Old Roger being ever able to ply
his fishing trade again. He had to sit idle in the chimney-corner, and
his carefully saved earnings were dwindling down until very little was
left.

His loving daughter told him not to trouble about it; she would work
hard enough to support him. But he knew she was already toiling far
beyond her strength, and this thought would creep in sometimes to dim
his bright faith.

Lettice strove hard to hide their fast-increasing poverty from Ermyn.
And it was not until one of the cows had fallen sick and died, and the
other had to be sold to a neighbouring farmer, that the good woman was
fairly overcome, and Ermyn found her sitting inside the doorway weeping
bitterly.

"Mother, darling mother!" said Ermyn, kneeling down beside her and
crying too. "Tell me what it is. Oh, let me help you if I can!"

So Lettice told her all, and hid none of the sad truth.

"Mother," cried the girl joyfully, "there is the box upstairs that you
have been putting all my little presents of money and savings into ever
since I was a little child. It is heavy now. We must open it and use
what is there. Oh, I am so glad you would not let me spend it before.
Davie put five shillings in last summer, and I will work and earn such
a lot more. I know I can. I will go up this afternoon and speak to Mrs.
Llewellyn about it."

"No," said Lettice, as a proud pang shot into her heart; "let us keep
our trouble to ourselves as long as we can."

Yet she felt she was wrong as the words fell from her lips.

"Oh, mother dear, why?" pleaded Ermyn. "She is so good and kind; do let
me go to her! I want her to tell me how I can best help you."

And the child begged so hard that at length she obtained a reluctant
consent.

Mrs. Llewellyn's heart opened in sympathy at once, and she reproached
herself for not having sooner thought of the scheme that now presented
itself to her mind. Ermyn was an exquisite fancy-worker, and under her
tuition had learned to do some very beautiful embroidery in coloured
silks. Mary had made many friends in London during her residence there,
and to some of these her mother proposed she should write, and try to
get orders for this work.

The appeal was responded to in a few days, and demands for the lovely
work flowed in faster than Ermyn's fingers could supply them. When the
first liberal payment in gold arrived, how proud and happy she felt
to take it home and refill the scanty purse, thus lifting some of the
pressure from the dear ones there.


Meantime, Lettice was learning her lesson. She had not been made aware
before how much pride lurked in her heart, and perhaps it was for
this reason God sent her a trial that laid it bare. She, the thrifty
housewife, who had always managed so well to make both ends meet, and
keep want far from her dwelling; she, who with Miss Silexa had so
often dispensed the alms from the Church funds, rejoicing that she
never stood in need of them herself; "she" to be poor and needy, and
obliged to put in her claim also at the Rectory for the dole of bread
and other necessaries that were given away at stated times to destitute
parishioners!

The flesh rebelled, but the spirit prevailed. It had been a subtle
snare, for she actually discovered she had been proud of her pride,
and it was not without a sore struggle in a strength not her own that
she conquered. Yet the chastened look on her sweet, patient face told
to those around that poverty had come as one of God's good angels, to
reveal unto her more fully the mysteries of His grace. Her cup was
still filled, because she held it under the Fountain of living water,
whose source can never fail, though earthly springs may run dry.

   "The barrel of meal wasted not, neither did the cruse of oil fail,
according to the word of the Lord."


One morning she was reading to her father the daily portion from her
precious Bible, and she came to the words,—

   "'Therefore I take pleasure in infirmities, in reproaches, in
necessities, in persecutions, in distresses for Christ's sake: for when
I am weak, then am I strong.'

"I thought," said Lettice humbly, "that we were good if we bore these
troubles patiently, and tried not to murmur; but these words go beyond
that, father. I have not taken pleasure in distresses for Christ's
sake; I have only striven to bear them in silence."

"Don't be afraid, my lass; our good Lord knows all about it—the
fightings without and the fears within; and He says,—

   "'My grace is sufficient for thee.'

"That means, it will meet every need and so fully satisfy us that we
shall be even joyful in tribulation. See what His grace did in the
prophet's heart when he could say,—

   "'Although the fig tree shall not blossom, neither shall fruit be in
the vines; the labour of the olive shall fail, and the fields shall
yield no meat; the flock shall be cut off from the fold, and there
shall be no herd in the stalls: yet I will rejoice in the Lord, I will
joy in the God of my salvation. The Lord God is my strength.'

"Now, my lass, we are not so badly off as that—praise be the Lord;
though He has seen fit to take away both the cows, there is yet meat in
the fields, for we never had a finer crop of potatoes than this year.
Things are never so bad but what they might be worse. There is an old
saying and a true one, 'God writes straight on crooked lines.'

"When I lie here upon my bed and count up all the Lord's mercies, why,
I can't number them, for—

   "'They are new every morning: and great is His faithfulness.'

   "'Bless the Lord, O my soul: and all that is within me, bless His holy
name. Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all His benefits:
who forgiveth all thine iniquities; who healeth all thy diseases;
who redeemeth thy life from destruction; who crowneth thee with
loving-kindness and tender mercies; who satisfieth thy mouth with good
things; so that thy youth is renewed like the eagle's.'

   "'Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation,
or distress, or persecution, or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or
sword? . . . Nay, in all these things we are more than conquerors
through Him that loved us.'"

The aged man clasped his hands together and looked upwards, lost in the
contemplation those holy words had awakened, and Lettice went to her
work, and there was music in her heart all the day long.


It was a grief to Ermyn that she could not be more at the cottage, but
she needed Mrs. Llewellyn's counsel and advice about her delicate work,
and the light was better for it in larger rooms. So she used to set out
as early as possible and not return until dusk, when she would bring
back with her a merry brightness that was like sunshine in the old home.

Lettice fondly hoped her father would struggle through the winter and
revive in the warm spring time. But as the days lengthened, she saw
with grief that his "strength was but labour and sorrow."

He sat less frequently in his chair, though sometimes she could
persuade him to have it placed just outside the door in the sunshine,
where he could feel the fresh breezes come up from the sea, and watch
the sailors among their boats. He seemed to enjoy it at the time, but
gradually this was too much for him, and he would lie down again on his
bed dreamy and quiet for hours, and soon grew too weak to leave it at
all.

One afternoon Lettice watched a tear slowly course down his
weather-beaten cheek, and she rose softly to smooth back the long white
locks from his noble forehead, saying tenderly, "What is it, father?
Are you in pain?"

"No, my lass, no," he answered, as a sweet smile broke over his face
like a glory; "the pain is all gone now, but the boat is ready, very
nearly ready, Lettie, my girl."

Lettice thought he was wandering, and said, "Do you want to go out in
your boat, father?"

"I am sailing away in it, my child, and my Lord the Pilot is going to
slip the cable soon. My face is set towards glory's shore. It won't be
long now before I am there, and I was crying to think of leaving you
behind."

Lettice took one long look into the dear, dim eyes, then laid her head
down on the pillow beside him, and sobbed her heart out there.

"Lettie! My Lettie! Listen to what He says,—

   "'I am with you alway, even unto the end of the world.'

   "'A Father of the fatherless and Judge of the widows is God in His holy
habitation.'

"Lettie, I leave you in His keeping,—

   "'and underneath are the everlasting arms.'"

The sacred words fell like balm upon her troubled spirit, and she heard
the voice saying once more within her soul,—

   "'Peace, peace, be still!'"

And there was a great calm.

A sound of footsteps in the outer room made her rise to see who had
entered. It was Mr. Williams, who stepped softly in, and holding out
his hand, asked kindly for her father.

The old man recognised his voice, and cried out:

"Come in, come in, sir. I'm so pleased to see you. Reach him a chair,
Lettice, and pull back the window curtain a bit farther, that he may
see what I have to look out upon. There!" he said, as his daughter
obeyed him. "There's the sea, rolling so grand and beautiful! God's
sea, sir," he added reverently, and with poetical fervour. "I love
to lie and watch it. The tide is fast coming in now: its ebbings and
flowings are sweet music in my ears. It makes me think of the voice of
many waters, and the harpers harping with their harps before the throne
of God on the crystal sea. I know the words of the new song they sing,
and I shall be joining with them soon."

"Yes," replied the young man, in a voice tremulous with emotion. "You
will soon see 'His face; and in His presence there is fulness of joy.'"

"Amen!" responded Roger fervently, as Mr. Williams noted in silence
what a change had come over the furrowed face since he had last seen
it. He had grown to love the old man dearly, as all did who came in
contact with him.

"Shall I send for David?" he asked huskily.

"No, sir, no; it will be too late, poor lad, now; for with to-morrow's
dawning, and perhaps before, I expect to—

   "'see the King in His beauty, and to behold the land that is very far
off.'

"I shall see none of them again now until the glorious resurrection
morning. Kind Miss Pryse, and the dear old parson, and that sweet
dove, Miss Eudora—they will all come back to find another grave in the
churchyard, but I shall be above."

Mr. Williams drew his Bible from his pocket, and read:

   "'Let not your heart be troubled: ye believe in God, believe also in
Me. In My Father's house are many mansions: if it were not so I would
have told you. I go to prepare a place for you. And if I go and prepare
a place for you, I will come again, and receive you unto Myself that
where I am, there ye may be also.'

   "'Father, I will that they also, whom Thou hast given Me, be with Me
where I am; that they may behold my glory, which Thou hast given Me:
for Thou lovedst Me before the foundation of the world.'"

Lettice was watching her father as he drank in the words, when she
suddenly saw him sink back on the pillow in a death-like swoon, and
both knew the end was even nearer than they thought.

"I have sent for Ermyn," whispered Lettice, as she applied
restoratives. "See, he is coming to himself again now."

"Where am I, Lettie?" said the old man faintly. "It was all so dark,
but I can see you again now."

"Yes, father dear, it is all right now, isn't it?"

"Ay, my lass—

   "'Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will
fear no evil: for Thou art with me; Thy rod and Thy staff they comfort
me.'"

   "'When thou passes through the waters, I will be with thee; and through
the rivers, they shall not overflow thee,—'"

whispered Mr. Williams.

Again there was a stir in the outer room, and Mary Llewellyn entered
with Ermyn at her side.

Roger knew Mary, and beckoned her nearer. Then, after the courteous
custom of the Welsh, he raised her hand to his lips and kissed it.
Ermyn drew near softly, and knelt down beside her mother close to the
bed, and many of Old Roger's sea-mates and neighbours came dropping in
one by one, until the cottage was nearly full.

"There stood by me this night the angel of God, whose I am, and whom I
serve," said the dying voice.

And all held their breath to listen.

"As He was with Saint Paul, so He will be with me.

   "'They sounded and found it twenty fathoms; and when they had gone a
little farther, they sounded again, and found it fifteen fathoms.'

"The water is getting shallower and shallower; there is land ahead!

   "'They feared lest they should have fallen upon rocks.'

"Ah! But I have no fear, for the Anchor of the soul both sure and
steadfast is mine, and it is fixed within the veil.

   "'They cast out the tackling of the ship'—

"That is, I take it, all my worldly affairs and cares. They are all
thrown overboard, and Christ alone is with me in the vessel. I see the
red harbour lights gleaming! They who sleep in Jesus rest well."

"Let us pray," said Mr. Williams, as the aged pilgrim ceased, exhausted
with the unnatural effort.

   "Unto God's gracious mercy and protection we commit thee; the Lord
bless thee and keep thee. The Lord make His face to shine upon thee,
and be gracious unto thee. The Lord lift up His countenance upon thee,
and give thee peace, both now and evermore. Amen."

There was no time for further words, for as the sunbeams streamed in
rosy and golden, and the waves washed up in a long farewell against the
boundary wall, a sigh, gentle as an infant's, loosed the silver cord,
and the golden bowl was broken. Old Roger had gone home to God.



CHAPTER XI

THE SAINTED WELL

THE sweet spring days had unfolded themselves into "the honeyed
heart of June," and "brakes with golden flowers were crowned." The
breath of morning rose in a purple haze, filled with the fragrance of
mountain-thyme and clover, and through the grass silver drops filtered
down to the thirsty roots below. The bright little beck hurried along
in tones of summer gladness, restless and eager to meet the tide as it
bounded in from the broad blue sea, and "there was a sparkling glee
where waves and sunshine met."

Ermyn stood on the bridge listening to the merry tinkle, then stepped
into the meadow beside the clear running water. Her pitcher was on the
bank, and after playing for some minutes with the pebbles in the bed
of the stream, she snatched it up and ran off in the direction of the
Sainted Well. The glory of earth and sky made her pause and linger; now
two brown butterflies flitted on before her; then she plucked a flower
here and grasses there; and now she followed with fascinated gaze the
white wings of the sea-gull circling above her head, until she found
they had lured her from the path, and she was still a considerable
distance from the Well. To make up for lost time, she climbed a loose
stone wall, and ran across some fields till she was on the right track
again, and the well was in sight.

She was breathless with the extra speed, and a rosy flush mounted to
her cheeks as she saw that some one was there before her. This was no
uncommon sight, it being such a favourite spot. But Ermyn was demure
in a moment, and steadying the pitcher on her shoulder, walked slowly
towards the spot.

St. Elvis was the resort of numerous visitors and tourists, especially
of artists, who might be seen almost every day through the summer with
canvas and easel, perched about on the hillsides or along the rocky
shore, trying to represent in glowing colours some of their uncommon
beauties.

The stranger looked up as he heard the light footsteps through the
rustling grass, and Ermyn saw before her a gentleman, who, from his
whitening hair and bent look, seemed past middle age. He had a long
beard and moustache, and the girl thought she had never beheld such a
thin, haggard face before, nor one so expressive of hopeless misery.

A sudden tremor filled her young heart, as the dark and piercing eyes
met hers, for, to her surprise, the artist turned pale and dropped his
palette. But she felt reassured when after stooping to pick it up, a
tender, mournful look came into his face, and he accosted her gently,—

"Good-morning; you are come to fetch some of this delicious water?"

"Yes, sir," replied Ermyn, curtseying as she spoke. "Would you please
that I should dip you up a little to drink?"

And, suiting the action to the word, she descended the red steps and
lowered her pitcher.

"You are ill, sir," she added, as the gentleman, having thanked her,
trembled visibly as he took the proffered water. "Our cottage is not
very far off, sir; and I am sure mother would get you a cup of tea if
you would come there and rest."

Ermyn had been trained from her childhood to offer the courteous
hospitality for which the Welsh are so famous.

"Thank you," he answered; "I will come, for I feel rather faint. I did
not take anything before I set out; but first let me beg you to stay a
few minutes. I should like to put you into this picture."

Ermyn looked down with a blush on her shabby dark blue print, which was
tucked up over an old red petticoat, and tossing back her hair from her
lace, she said simply, "My Sunday gown is better than this, sir."

"I would rather have this one, please," he rejoined, with a mournful
smile. "Can you hold your pitcher on your shoulder? So—I will not keep
you long."

Ermyn assented with a shy laugh, and stood perfectly still, wondering
much, as she looked towards the cliffs, why this sad-looking man should
care to paint her; for our maiden of the valley had not yet been
spoiled by being told she had a lovely face.

While the artist sketched, he drew her into conversation. "Have you
always lived in St. Elvis?"

"We do not live in St. Elvis, sir, but at Porthmynwr, though it is
reckoned all one, I believe. We live at the cottage that stands close
at the head of the creek. There is only mother and me now, for Davie is
away, and dear old granddad died in the early spring."

"Who is Davie?"

"He is my brother, sir, and we had a baby sister long ago who died; she
was called little Ruth. Mother has only me now."

"And what is 'your' name?"

"Ermyn Silexa Gwynne," replied the girl promptly.

"I cannot paint any more this morning," said the stranger abruptly, as,
rising, he gathered up his materials and prepared to follow his guide.

During their walk the conversation was resumed.

"Do you know why you were given that name?"

"I suppose, sir, because Miss Pryse is my godmother."

And Ermyn then proceeded to inform him who lived at the Rectory, and
told him, in fact, all she knew in connection with her own history.

The artist paused every now and then to rest his burden, and Ermyn
wondered why he questioned her so closely.

He was silently connecting this morning's scene with one of the
previous evening, when he had arrived by the coach at St. Elvis.

No one knew who the stranger was; and the villagers eagerly scanned
both him and his luggage, which, however, had no name on it. This fact
was observed as it was carried up to the room he had engaged at "The
Jolly Sailors." Many pairs of eyes from the stone cross steps followed
him inquisitively as he sauntered down towards the Rectory gate and
leaned over its palings. But he was only a stranger among strangers, as
he watched Molly walk across the grass with a basket of clothes on her
arm, which she had been gathering in from the common close by. A young
clergyman was talking to a lady at the wild porch, but he did not know
any of them.

"I suppose they are all dead," he thought.

And after a long wistful look at the casements buried in their mass of
green, he walked slowly away, carrying the sore burden of a heart heavy
as lead. Why did the conversation with this simple village maid cause
his heart to bound madly, as the colour mounted into his sunken cheek?

"It is impossible!" he thought. "Why will such fancies come? The letter
said the child died too. But oh, why is this girl so like my lovely
Ethel? My morbid brain conjures up nothing but phantoms. But my father
lives! I must see his face once more before I die. And Silexa! How she
loved me! And yet she was forbidden ever to write or speak to me again.
My punishment has been greater than I could bear. God is not a God of
love, but a stern avenger, and the greatest boon I crave now from His
hand is to die."

"There is mother!" cried Ermyn, as they neared the cottage.

And Lettice came forward to question her child anxiously on the reason
of her delay.

"I must bear the blame," said the stranger quickly. "I have been trying
to take a sketch of your daughter at the Sainted Well."

"Yes; and, mother, will you please give the gentleman a cup of tea? He
is ill. I must run away and make myself tidy, for Mrs. Llewellyn will
be waiting for me. Good-morning, sir."

"Good-bye," said the visitor; "will you let me go on with the picture
to-morrow?"

"Oh yes, sir, if you like."

And the girl ran off.

"Will you please to come inside, sir, while I get you something?" said
the wondering Lettice. "May I ask if you have travelled far to-day?"

"No; I have only come from St. Elvis, where I slept last night.
Will you allow me to rest under the shadow of this hawthorn? What a
beautiful child your daughter is!"

"Yes, she is well enough," answered Lettice stiffly; for she was always
dubious whither such remarks might tend.

"She is so like some one who was very dear to me, and who died; that is
why I wanted to take her likeness."

"Indeed, sir!"

"Is she your own child?" asked the stranger, in a low tone.

But he was not prepared to see Lettice start and turn very pale as she
answered.

"No, she is not, but she does not know it. All the neighbours and
townsfolk have been kind enough to keep my secret, and I shall never
tell her. She is just the same as if she were my own, for I have had
her ever since she was a little baby."

"How did you get her?" pursued her questioner, with difficulty
concealing his agitation.

"Well, sir, 'tis a sad tale. Rather more than twelve years ago a fine
ship, called the 'Ocean Queen,' foundered on yonder reef of rocks,
and all hands were drowned. None were saved except this poor little
helpless babe, who was found lashed to a spar and brought home by my
father. The mother of the infant was cold in death, but one arm was
round her baby when they found her. The sailors carried her into the
cottage, where she lay until after the inquest. And on the following
Sunday, we buried her in the old churchyard at St. Elvis."

"Does the child bear any resemblance to the mother?"

"Well, sir, I sometimes fancy she does; but features change after
death, so I can scarcely tell. Do you know anything about the wreck,
sir?"

"No, nothing; I never even heard of it before."

Lettice felt relieved.

"Then this stranger can surely have no claim on her," she thought, "but
I must be careful what I say. I will not tell him about the locket, and
I will never give her up to any one who cannot describe it accurately
before seeing it."

Poor gentleman! She felt very sorry for him; he looked so forlorn and
ill. He evidently had a sad history attached to him. And as the artist
raised his dark eyes to hers, a remembrance of long ago flashed into
her mind, but faded away before it could take a definite form.

"Do you make a long stay here, sir?" she asked presently.

"No; I go the next time the coach leaves St. Elvis. I suppose there is
no other means of communication with the market town?"

"Not unless you order a carriage from there to fetch you, sir, but that
is expensive," she added thoughtfully, looking at his shabby clothes.
"The coach does not run again this week, sir."

"Then I must hire," he replied; "for there is no time to be lost. There
is foul play somewhere."

Lettice wondered why he said this, but she did not remark on it.

"I will come again to-morrow, if you will allow me," he said earnestly.
"I should like to finish my picture of the child."

"Oh, surely, sir, you are very welcome; I will tell her about it."

A pause, and then the stranger asked abruptly, "Where was she buried?"

"The poor lady, sir? For she 'was' a lady, I'm sure. Oh, it was up in
the corner of the churchyard, by the sycamore trees, sir. If you would
please wait here a few minutes, I could come with you and show you, and
perhaps you would like to see the church also?"

"Thank you, I should; I will wait here until you are ready."

They were soon walking along the meadows, white and golden with
daisies and buttercups, towards the old town. And as they went, the
conversation turned upon the Rectory.

"I knew Dr. Pryse long ago," said the stranger, "and consider myself an
old friend of the family."

"Indeed, sir; then of course you know about their trouble? Our dear
pastor was aged before his time; he had a sorrow, sir, that well-nigh
killed him."

"What was it?" said her listener.

Lettice paused before answering. It was unlike her to talk much of the
affairs of others. Yet this gentleman was a friend of the family, and
there was something about him which told her his question sprang not
from impertinent curiosity.

"There could surely," she thought, "be no harm in telling what all the
village knew."

"His only son, sir," she said at length, "such a fine, noble youth,
his father's joy and pride; there were few that could show beside him
for beauty, sir. He went away to London to his father's friend, Mr.
Lyndwood, who, having no children of his own, offered to take him into
his office and provide for him. Well, sir, I suppose he fell in with
bad companions. I don't know, but I always will say I don't believe he
meant to steal the money, and Miss Silexa has always said the same.
We suppose he got into debt, and was afraid to tell his father, who
was a terribly stern man at that time, over strict, sir, with all his
children. But I think he learned his lesson when it was too late.

"Well, the news came one morning that his son had stolen bank notes to
the value of two hundred pounds, and had disappeared. I shall never
forget the father's rage. I was up at the Rectory at the time. He said
he would never see his face again, and forbade Miss Pryse to have any
communication with her brother. Soon after a letter came from Master
Edward, but giving no address, begging his father to pay the money,
and promising in the future to try and amend the past. A reply by
advertisement was requested.

"Dr. Pryse took no notice of the letter, expecting if he waited long
enough, the lad would come meekly back, and ask his forgiveness. But
Miss Silexa knew it was not like Master Edward to do that—he had such a
terribly high spirit; and she used to come down to our cottage and weep
her heart out about it. Poor lady! She has had a troubled life of it
since in many ways."

"And did the son write again?" asked the artist.

But Lettice did not perceive how he trembled.

"No, sir, at least not then; and as the years went on, his poor father
seemed to bend lower and lower under the grief. He would have given all
he had to bring him back. But though he and his friend Mr. Lyndwood
made every inquiry, no tidings could be heard that would give the
shadow of a hope. Why, it must be five-and-twenty years ago since he
went away. How time flies!"

"Do you think the old man has ever forgiven his son?"

"Oh yes, sir. I am sure he has; he has learned too much of God's love
himself to deny forgiveness to his son. I am certain, if he had come
back, he would have fallen on his neck and kissed him, and killed for
him the fatted calf. He can never read that chapter in church about
David's lament over Absalom without breaking down."

"I suppose he has given up all hope now?" rejoined her auditor, in a
questioning tone.

"Oh yes, sir; they all think, now, Master Edward must have died
somewhere abroad, though, they began to hope again after they got the
letter."

"I thought you said there was no letter."

"Not from him, sir. But twelve years after he went away, Mr. Lyndwood
received the money again, with no name or address. He has been doing
all he can ever since to find out where it came from."

"How very strange!" replied her questioner. "And so they are all gone
away now?" he added. "Who is the young lady your daughter was telling
me about?"

"Miss Eudora Meredith, sir, the child of Miss Silexa's sister,
beautiful Miss Minnie Pryse, the pride of St. Elvis. She died within a
year of her marriage. There is a sad story about that, sir. You must
surely have heard it, if you are a friend of the family."

The stranger started, fearing he had betrayed himself. But the movement
was unperceived by the innocent, simple-hearted woman, who waited for
the answer.

"No, I have heard nothing. My home has been out of England for many
years. Do tell me all you know, as I shall not see any of them myself
now."

"True, sir; they will not be back until the fall of the year. I am
sorry you will miss them. Well, I will tell you about Mrs. Meredith.
She had more schooling than her elder sister, Miss Pryse; for there
was always so much to be done at home that somehow she could not be
spared, and their poor mother was an invalid for years before she died.
Miss Minnie was one of the wildest, merriest-hearted girls I ever saw;
a regular little mischievous one, always up to pranks. She was named
after Dame Ermyn Brande, who was an ancestor of the Pryse family. You
will see her monument directly over the Rectory pew, telling of her
bequest to St. Elvis, but the child was never called anything but
Minnie. After the death of Mrs. Pryse, she was sent away to school for,
I think, five or six years. It was a long way off, so she seldom came
home until she finished.

"And while she was absent, Captain Edward Meredith came to visit at the
Rectory. His ship, the 'Eudora,' was anchored in the bay. Oh, but he
'was' a fine gentleman! I always say I never saw one as handsome except
our Master Edward of the Rectory. Captain Meredith was, I believe, a
year or two younger than Miss Pryse, but no one would have guessed it.
They looked such a fine handsome couple as they walked about together;
and everybody in St. Elvis and Porthmynwr thought it was going to be a
match between them.

"I have seen Miss Silexa, when he used to be absent, most days walk
along the cliff and shore, as if eagerly watching for his ship; and
whenever his name was mentioned, she would look up to listen. If ever
a man might have won a noble heart, it was he, sir, but perhaps he
didn't know. Miss Silexa was a proud young woman, and never betrayed
her feelings, but I had not seen such a glad look on her face since the
day our Master Edward went away. I hoped she was going to find comfort
in her affliction by becoming a happy wife."

"And how did it end?" said the stranger, pausing to take breath.

"It ended, sir, one summer when the 'Eudora' was lying out there in the
bay at anchor. Miss Minnie had just returned from school, and every one
was talking of her great beauty. I shall never forget the first Sunday
I saw her come into church, with her blue eyes and golden hair, smiling
like an angel. Captain Meredith went into the Rectory pew, and, I
suppose, soon fell in love, for he was always with her after that, and
in a little while, we heard they were engaged. They were married in the
following spring, quite early in the year. They went away together for
a trip somewhere, and then he had to bring her home here and leave her,
as his ship was ordered away suddenly to foreign parts. His poor little
wife drooped for him, and by degrees the rosy colour faded out of her
cheeks.

"One November morning, I was sent for in haste to the Rectory. And my
heart ached as I went over the threshold and heard the sound of bitter
weeping. Miss Silexa met me with the newspaper in her hand, looking as
pale as ashes. The 'Eudora' had gone down, and all in her had perished,
except two sailors, who escaped to bring the news to land.

"Our beautiful Miss Minnie never looked up again. I stayed there all
day, and in the evening, she gave birth to her little daughter. When
she heard the baby's cry, she whispered to her sister,—

"'Call her Eudora, and take care of her for Edward's sake. I am going
to him.'

"We tried by every means to rouse her, but she just sank away like a
tired child going to sleep. I thought the double sorrow would have
killed poor Dr. Pryse. We could not get him to leave the body; he sat
moaning over it, holding the waxen hand in his, and crying out,—

"'My little Minnie! Look at me once more! Wake up, my darling!'"

"Miss Silexa did not shed a tear, but seemed turned into stone, as she
sat rocking the poor baby on her knees. And she has been a mother to
her ever since."

"It is indeed a sad story," said the gentleman, turning his back to
Lettice, and stooping to gather a flower, by which action he managed to
hide the tears that had filled his eyes. "The child must be a precious
treasure," he added.

"Oh, sir, the old man dotes on her, and so does Miss Silexa; please
God she may be spared to them! They nearly lost her last year when
she was ill. She is very like her mother, only without her beautiful
colour. I wish they were all home again. I think that is all I have
to tell you, sir. And I see old John Jenkins coming. He will show you
over the church. Step this way first, sir; here is the grave, and Mrs.
Meredith's is next to it. I will wish you good-morning. Oh no, sir; I
did not expect payment," she added hastily, as the stranger slipped a
sovereign into her hand.

"Please accept it with my best thanks," he urged, in reply to her
remonstrances, as an earnest look came into his dark eyes; "I will be
at your cottage to-morrow."

There was no headstone to mark the resting-place of the drowned one;
only the long grasses waved pleasantly over it, and there was a hushing
sound in the sycamore branches above. He knelt beside it to pluck a few
green blades, which he put into his pocket-book. He then passed on to
look at the headstone next to it.

"So young to die!" he murmured, on reading the inscription.

"Would you please to go into the church, sir?" asked John Jenkins, who
had come close to where he was standing. "'Tis a fine old church, folks
say, sir," he added, as the gentleman turned to accompany him. "This
doorway is Norman, and the tower is hundreds of years old, and I can
show you some very ancient monuments."

The old pew-opener was proceeding to descant on these, when, to his
surprise, he found the stranger had left him, and was now far ahead of
him up the church aisle, and just turning into the Rectory pew, where
he sat down, and was lost to view.

Old Jenkins did not follow him there, or he would have been still more
astonished to find him sitting in one corner among the fusty cushions,
with his head bowed low upon his hands, shedding, silently, bitter
tears.

"A curious sort of a chap that!" thought the verger, as ten minutes
later the gentleman reappeared, and putting some money in his hand
abruptly left the church.


The artist kept his appointment the following morning, and finished the
picture.

"Look, mother, what the gentleman gave me!" cried Ermyn, as she ran
into the cottage. "A whole sovereign! Is it enough to buy a cow? You
must take it and use it. Oh, we shall be rich some day," she added,
kissing her. "I am doing such a beautiful piece of work now. A wreath
of wild roses on white silk. Mrs. Llewellyn says it is the prettiest I
ever did."

"It is far too much money, my child; I am sorry he gave it, for he
seems poor himself, and I grieve from my heart for him. He looks as if
life had pressed sore upon him with its burden."

St. Elvis opened its eyes in considerable astonishment the following
morning, when a carriage and pair arrived from the market town, and the
stranger stepped into it and drove off.

"I thought he was one of your poor travelling artists," remarked the
ostler, who had shouldered the well-worn portmanteau.

"He paid his reckoning like a gentleman, anyhow," rejoined the
landlady, "but I never saw a shabbier kit."

"He looked as if he hadn't a sixpence in the world to call his own,"
added a bystander; "and 'what' a hat!"

Molly was welcomed eagerly at the stone cross that morning, for she had
something else to tell about the nameless and mysterious stranger.

"He was up late last night," she said, "prowling round the Rectory
like a thief, peeping into all the windows and under the willow tree
and all. And he picked the flowers, and actually went into the kitchen
garden opposite, as if he had a right there. I didn't like to tell him
he mustn't go there."

"Well, I never!" nodded the gossips, with upraised hands. "It was as
well Miss Pryse wasn't at home. She would have ordered him off sharp.
She don't like strangers about."

But none of these remarks reached the ear of our traveller, who was
speeding on his way as fast as a pair of fleet horses could carry him
to the nearest railway station, whence he was soon flying along in the
express train towards Scotland.



CHAPTER XII

THE LAIRD OF MUIRSTANE

THE long Scotch twilight was rapidly fading, and lights glimmered here
and there from the windows of the town, towards which four tired horses
were dragging the public conveyance after a hard day's journey. There
was but one inn of any size in the place, and this was homely both
in style and appearance, notwithstanding the fierce attitude of the
so-called "Golden Lion" swinging on the signboard above the entrance.

As the coach crossed the last bridge, and rattled over the ill-paved
streets, a crowd of barefooted urchins set up a series of yells, and
commenced running after it in a body, clamouring and squabbling for
the halfpence a traveller threw down for their benefit. At the sound
of these noisy heralds, two ostlers ran out from the inn-yard, who
were speedily joined by other hangers-on. The stout landlady appeared
resplendent in plaid ribbons, sinning benignly; and many heads bobbed
up and down behind her, all eager to see what the tired tourists were
like.

The poor artist whom St. Elvis had in turn looked down upon and
wondered at, was among them; though this fact might have been doubted,
as he had exchanged his shabby attire for a respectable suit, and
certainly looked much more like a gentleman as he descended the
step-ladder and accosted Mrs. Macgregor:

"Can I have a fresh pair of horses to take me to Muirstane to-night?"

"'Deed no, sir; ye can ha'e no horses the nicht, ye maun e'en wait till
the morn. We ha'e been ower busy here this week past, and the Duke had
the last pair this afternoon to gang to the castle. I'm sorry, sir, but
ye maun put up wi' us, and tak' the best oor hoose can gi'e ye."

Our traveller remonstrated, but finding it was useless, he gave orders
for his luggage to be carried in, and was glad to escape from the
noise and confusion outside to the parlour within. A smell of beer and
whisky, mingled with the fumes of stale tobacco, made him feel sick and
giddy, after all his fatigue. It was not much better upstairs, for the
inn being unusually crowded, he had to be content with a small attic,
whose window could only be opened a few inches by a desperate effort,
after which a wedge of wood had to be inserted to keep the sash from
falling again.

He inquired how soon his supper could be ready; and being told "in
about half an hour," he went out into the cool night air while it was
preparing. Fresh breezes came up from the river as he leant over the
stone bridge, and watched the reflection of stars in the clear water
beneath. The hills were faintly outlined in the distance, and there was
a soughing in the belt of Scotch pines that skirted the bend of the
stream.

But the soft whispers brought no rest nor peace to the weary one who
stood there, absently gazing at the dim landscape. He could only review
the sad past, to which was now added his strange visit to St. Elvis,
and what he had seen and heard there. Since then, rapid journeys by
day and night had finally brought him to this place, where he was now
forcibly detained by circumstances.

"I wish I had never seen that child," he muttered, as he retraced his
footsteps to the house.

And with a wilful inconsistency, climbed the stairs again to unlock
his portmanteau and take out Ermyn's picture. He looked at the face he
saw smiling there, comparing it with a miniature he always wore, and
carried it downstairs with him.

Two dismal tallow-candles were spluttering away on a table in the fusty
little room where he found his supper awaiting him. The meal consisted
of fried eggs and ham, some excellent coffee, with plenty of good bread
and butter. And in spite of his sorrow and disappointment, the tired
wayfarer enjoyed it.

After the remains of the feast had been duly cleared away by a
red-handed and red-haired serving-maid, he produced the pictures again,
and laying them carefully on the table before him, took another long
look, murmuring to himself, "They 'are' alike."

Waking from his reverie, he proceeded to investigate the room. The
bookcase contained a Scotch psalm-book, the "Shorter Catechism,"
Bunyan's "Holy War" and "Pilgrim's Progress," a stray novel or two
left by passing visitors, some "Chambers's Journals," well-thumbed and
dog-eared, and a collection of old "Illustrated London News," bound in
a cloth cover and considerably the worse for wear. The last-mentioned
volume had evidently been a favourite study of previous guests, as was
only too apparent from the tattered condition of its leaves, and the
dirty marks visible upon them.

Edward Pryse (for our readers will have guessed ere this that it was
he) turned over the pages mechanically, thinking of far different
subjects, when suddenly his eye was attracted by the picture of a
ship. He started, and his heart thumped against his breast as he read
the words underneath it—"The Ocean Queen." With trembling fingers, he
turned to the paragraph to which it belonged, and pursued the following
communication:—

   "'Loss of the ship 'Ocean Queen,' paddle-steamer, bound from Liverpool
to Cork. She broke her paddle-shaft in a gale of wind in St. George's
Channel, and drifted on to St. Dewis Head, on October 7, and became a
total wreck. Crew and passengers all lost.'"

"What a strange coincidence! How this shipwreck haunts me!" he cried,
as the letters swam before his eyes, and he gasped for breath.

It was all a dream! But no, there it was, staring him in the face.
True, quite true! He rose and paced the room until he grew calmer.
Then, sitting down again, he drew a penknife from his pocket and slowly
and deliberately cut out the page, folded it carefully, and laid it
with the picture. He then restored the book to its dusty quarters on
the shelf, rang for his bedroom candle, and went upstairs.

The landlady passed him on his way, and he felt quite guilty. But she
only smiled as she wished him "Gude-nicht," adding, "ye'll no' mind if
ye hear a hubbub aboot the hoose; it'll be just oorsels."

Edward paced long up and down the little room, too excited to seek
repose.

At last he said, half aloud, "I must write to Liverpool and inquire if
any lady of the name of Pryse sailed in that vessel."

After this was done, being completely worn-out by his previous
journeys, he flung himself on his bed and slept soundly.


While waiting to start on his journey the following morning, two lines
that he had read somewhere long ago came into his mind, and haunted it
all through that day:

   "There's a Divinity that shapes our ends,
    Rough-hew them how we will."

His destination was many miles off, and the road lay through some of
the most beautiful scenery Scotland can produce. His artistic eye did
not fail to note the glories of the landscape on either hand. When
they stopped at a small village to rest and bait the horses, he made
his way down to a rocky dell, attracted thither by the brawling of a
brown sunlit river. The waters fell sheer down with a thundering sound,
and on the large mossy boulder above, whence they took the final leap,
they were gathered up in a shining mass, which, on reaching the lower
stones, sprang off in silver threads of spray, while fairy fountains
were made rainbow-coloured and brilliant by the sunlight that glinted
through the over-arching branches above.

But he could not stay long in this enchanted spot. He must hurry on
to his journey's end, and it was the thought of what he should meet
with there that weighted his heart with lead. His last pilgrimage
to Muirstane had been a fruitless one, and in all probability this
would be the same. He was fully prepared for it, as in the light of
a glorious setting sun, he saw the roofs of the Highland village
appearing in view.

"I 'will' gain admittance this time," he muttered, with a bitter smile,
as, having left the carriage at the inn, he walked slowly up a steep
hill towards a large house, visible for many miles round in its utter
loneliness and isolation.

The huge iron gates looked as if they had not swung on their hinges for
a century, for they were coated with thick rust, and clustering weeds
and ivy clung about them. Edward Pryse touched the chain that hung from
the padlock. It clanked drearily as he let it drop again, and went
round to the dingy back entrance, through a court where grass grew rank
among the paving-stones. Here he pulled a wooden handle, which set a
bell ringing somewhere far-away in a distant passage.

No answer.

After waiting some minutes in impatient suspense, he tried the door. It
yielded to his vigorous push, and Edward found himself at the end of
a long dark corridor. On this side of the house there was no sign of
life, and the unexpected visitor made his way in the direction of the
inhabited end.

He reached the foot of a stair leading, as he well remembered, to
a little sitting-room, which had been Ethel's favourite retreat.
Ascending the steps, he knocked loudly at the door of the room, but
again without reply. Led by irresistible impulse, he entered.

How familiar, yet strange, the place appeared, in the thronging
memories of happy hours spent in that room by her side! The room
seemed exactly as it used to be, only all things wore a deathly look,
because the spirit that had given them life had fled. A harp stood
in one corner, but the strings hung broken and useless, and all the
gilding was worn away. A picture of his own painting hung above it, and
underneath the frame these words were illuminated,—

   "'God is our refuge and strength: a very present help in trouble.'"

A large old-fashioned rosewood workbox stood on a dusty round table in
the window. Edward opened it, and some dried rose-leaves gave forth
their peculiar odour as he lifted the lid and took out the tray. Oh,
what did he see beneath? A tiny half-finished infant's shirt of finest
cambric. The needle was still in it, but so rusty, it had imprinted an
orange stain on the fabric, now yellow with age. Tears filled his eyes;
he handled and kissed it reverently, then folding it together put it
into his breast-pocket, and closed the lid again.

Were there any other precious mementoes of his lost one? Yes, some of
her books were there, with a thick coating of dust resting on them. One
lay apart from the others; he opened it first, and some verbena and ivy
leaves fluttered down to his feet. It was Baxter's "Saints' Everlasting
Rest." The words his eye fell on were these, and they were underlined
with pencil:

   "When should we take our cordials, but in times of fainting? When is it
more seasonable to walk to heaven, than when we know not in what corner
of the earth to live with comfort? or when should our thoughts converse
more above, than when they have nothing but grief below? Where should
Noah's dove be but in the ark, when the waters cover all the earth, and
she cannot find rest for the sole of her foot? What should we think on,
but our Father's house, when we have not even the husks of the world to
feed upon? Surely God sends thy affliction for this very purpose."

He turned the pages and read again:

   "Hast thou seen the utter insufficiency of every creature, either to
be itself thy happiness or the means of removing this thy misery?
Hast thou been convinced, that thy happiness is only in God as the
end, and in Christ as the way to Him? and that thou must be brought
to God through Christ or perish eternally? Hast thou seen an absolute
necessity of thy enjoying Christ and a full sufficiency in Him to
do for thee whatever thy case requires? Hast thou discovered the
excellence of this pearl to be worth thy selling all to buy it?"

Edward Pryse could read no further for the dimming tears.

"No, my God," he groaned, "I have not! I found an earthly pearl,
beautiful and rare, and she was my idol; and Thou hast taken her from
me. Is it that I may find Thee, the Pearl of greatest price? Oh, my
sweet dove, that flitted 'twixt earth and sky o'er stormy seas! God put
forth His hand and drew thee in to rest with Him for evermore. Thou art
safe sheltered on His breast; and the world outside is cold without
thee. I shall go to thee, but thou canst not return to me."

A sound of footsteps in the corridor outside made him start.

And in another moment, an old woman, of forbidding appearance,
presented herself, and demanded his business.

"My name is Mr. Edward Pryse, and I am here to see your master."

"You know he will not see you; besides, he is very ill, and must not be
disturbed. How dared you enter? Here, John! John!"

At the call, a man came swiftly along the passage, leaving at the other
end a door ajar.

"Come, be off!" he said menacingly, but shrunk back in fear as Edward
advanced towards him.

"Will you show me your master's room?" sternly demanded the unwelcome
guest.

Before the housekeeper and her husband could frame a stubborn negative,
Edward thought he heard a faint sound, as of an old man in pain,
proceed from the room they had quitted. Did his ears deceive him? Or
was there a faint cry of "Edward! Edward!"

He rushed past the ill-favoured pair, and passed through the anteroom,
whence they had issued, into the bedchamber beyond. What a sight met
his view! In a tattered shirt, and lying on the bare mattress, with a
ragged blanket to cover him, lay the pitiable old man, his limbs and
features shrunk and attenuated to the last degree, and shaking from
head to foot.

"You have only come just in time to see me die! Why didn't you come
sooner?"

"I have been here at two different times, but could not gain
admittance. And judging from your last letter to me, I thought you
never wished to see my face again. It was a cruel letter."

"God forgive me!" murmured the old man. "I have deeply sinned. He 'may'
forgive me, but you never, when you know all!"

"I 'will' forgive, if you will only speak, and tell me 'all.'"

"I lied to you," said the helpless victim of cunning and avarice; "I
hated you for gaining Ethel's affections, when I had destined her to be
the wife of a rich neighbour. And when you went away, I swore I would
be revenged."

Edward grew pale as death, but was silent.

"She ran away," continued the narrator, "and I don't know where she
went. I suppose she was driven to it, and hoped to find you somewhere
in the wide world. I forced her, by keeping her short of money, to
leave the cottage you took for her in the village, and to return here.
I made her life in this house a hard and bitter bondage."

"But why did she not write to me? I sent her letters and remittances
regularly, but they were never acknowledged."

"Oh, Edward, forgive me, the hand of death is on me! I intercepted both
her letters and yours. I bribed the postmistress to let me have all
communications addressed to Ethel from America, and all hers to you.
They are all tied together in my desk, and you shall have them now. Ah!
Curse me not, I entreat you," added the unhappy man, as Edward uttered
a hasty exclamation. "God's curse has rested upon me so heavily that it
cannot be worse. Hear me to the end, and then help me to make my peace
with the Almighty, against whom I have sinned so deeply. The letter
I sent to you announcing her death and that of the child was false.
I never heard of her from the day she disappeared. I did it that you
should not expect my money."

"She 'did' not die, then!" said Edward, starting to his feet, not
heeding the last sentence. "Where is she, and what of her child?"

"It was not born here," replied the wretched miser; "but she was
preparing for it when she fled from the house. Go and look for her,
Edward; she must be somewhere in the world. I must see her again, and
ask her forgiveness before I die, or how can I meet her father and
mother, who left the friendless orphan to me, her only relative?"

"But had you no idea where she went?"

"In my anger I did not care. I thought surely her want of money must
soon drive her back. And when later I sought to find her, all trace was
gone. When people inquired for her, I said she had gone to you. Since
she left me, these thieves have gradually robbed me of everything they
could lay their hands upon, and now that I am ill and helpless, you see
how they have treated me."

"Why did you not send the wretches out of your house? How came you to
put your neck under their feet?"

"They had my secret, Edward, and they used their power cruelly. I was
afraid to let you enter the house for fear they would betray me. They
extorted larger and larger bribes, and when I remonstrated, threatened
to tell you all. At last, in my misery, I decided to throw myself on
your mercy, but did not know where to send for you. The woman said she
knew, and promised to send, but I didn't believe she would."

Edward was about to reply, when the old man laid his hand on his arm,
and said in a hollow whisper: "You must find Ethel; I have willed the
whole of my large property to her, and her child after her. See," he
added, gasping, "you will find the will in a secret drawer, just inside
the panel of—"

Edward never heard the remainder of the sentence, for a paralytic
stroke had fallen on the aged sinner, and he lay helpless and
unconscious.

To ring the bell and call loudly for assistance was Edward's first act,
but no one responded to his appeal.

He ran through room after room, and found all empty and deserted.
The man and his wife had made off in the interval, terrified at the
consequences of their iniquitous behaviour. Edward opened doors, and
flew along corridors, and finally found himself in the paved court
below. Here he saw a man passing the gate, and recognising him as an
inhabitant of Muirstane, thrust some money into his hand, and bade
him fetch a doctor as quickly as possible, and send help up from the
village.

The man was as good as his word, and soon summoned the needful
assistance. Everything that human skill or kindness could devise was
done to relieve the sufferer. Edward Pryse sat with him day and night.
And when a glimmering consciousness returned without the power of
speech, Ethel's uncle would lie looking wistfully at him, holding his
hand in his, and vainly moving his lips, as if trying to articulate
something that he had on his mind, but no sound ever came.

Edward fancied the old man understood what he said to him. And one day
as he sat beside him, the thought would itself upon his mind, "Is his
guilty soul sheltered from the wrath of the avenging Judge?"

Mr. Pryse was not a religious man himself, and he felt it, as death
knocked grimly at this chamber door. He had allowed the circumstances
of his life to harden him against God. But the words to which his dead
Ethel had so unconsciously pointed him, in her little red book, clung
to him, as if the good seed must find a resting-place even in "his"
stony heart.

He went softly down to her bookcase again, and found a Bible. He did
not know where to read, but again the marked passages came to his aid,
as he sat down to find something to comfort her uncle with. Slowly
turning the pages to and fro, he read aloud wherever he saw the traces
of her pencil—

   "'When Jesus heard it, He saith unto them, They that are whole have no
need of the physician, but they that are sick: I came not to call the
righteous, but sinners to repentance.'

   "'Come now, and let us reason together, saith the Lord: though your
sins be as scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they be red
like crimson, they shall be as wool.'

   "'God commendeth His love toward us, in that, while we were yet
sinners, Christ died for us. Much more then, being now justified by His
blood, we shall be saved from wrath through Him.'

   "'Behold the Lamb of God, which taketh away the sin of the world.'"

Edward fancied he saw a movement of the pale, shrivelled lips, as the
eyes looked up towards heaven with a supplicating glance, but that was
all. After a few more weary struggles, the soul that had sinned so
deeply went forth to appear before a higher tribunal than man's.

"God help me," ejaculated Edward, "to forgive him from my heart! 'I'
forgive him for Ethel's sake."

And he turned sadly away to give directions to the attendants who stood
by.



CHAPTER XIII

BAFFLED HOPES

EDWARD PRYSE followed the remains of his wife's relative to the
grave as chief and only mourner. No one came forward then to claim
kinship with the lonely and unloved old man. But as nearly the whole
of Muirstane belonged to him, a goodly number of tenants joined the
funeral procession. Already they looked upon Edward as their new
master, and accordingly began to pay court to him, hoping they would
find in him a more indulgent landlord. Not a few were eager to inform
him of facts they would not have cared to make public during Mr.
Macdonald's lifetime, and willingly gave him all the assistance in
their power, while he made a careful and patient investigation of every
matter respecting his wife.

The letter he wrote to Liverpool had only brought a curt reply that
