no lady of the name of Pryse, or of the description he had given, had
sailed in the "Ocean Queen." But the belief that Ermyn must be Ethel's
child had taken too strong a hold of him to be entirely set aside even
by this. He must seek for some other clue. But in the meantime, it was
his duty to turn from this engrossing matter to the immediate necessity
of settling his uncle's affairs.

Mr. Tod, the lawyer, who had drawn up the hidden document, was absent
from home at the time of Mr. Macdonald's decease, but returned with all
speed in answer to Edward's summons. Mr. Pryse informed him accurately
of all that had taken place since his arrival at Muirstane, to which
the lawyer listened in professional silence. When everything had been
stated, Mr. Tod asked the following question slowly and deliberately:

"Then if this document is found—and there can be no doubt of its
existence, from all you tell me and from what I know myself—can you
throw any light as to the whereabouts of the unfortunate lady, his
niece, who is entitled to this property under her uncle's gift? Or, if
she is dead, whether she left a child to take it?"

"I have reason to believe," replied Edward, "that my wife, the lady
you refer to, whom I married as Ethel Macdonald, was drowned in the
steamer 'Ocean Queen,' which was wrecked in the St. George's Channel in
the year 18—. But I have yet to prove that this was the case. If I am
right in my conjecture, I can produce her only child, a girl, whom I
accidentally discovered living under another name in South Wales. She
is supposed to be the daughter of a poor woman, who, however, confessed
to me that the girl was not her own child."

The lawyer expressed much surprise at this piece of information, and
promised all the help he could give towards unravelling the interesting
mystery, and ways and means were accordingly discussed most earnestly.
However, the disquisition ended by their coming back to the point they
started from: the document must be found first, before any other steps
could be taken.

Meantime, efforts were made to track the guilty fugitives, who had
departed immediately Edward had closed the doors upon them. Thanks
to the stupidity of the thieves, some valuable property was soon
recovered. But it needed a long and careful search before the man
and his wife were finally captured. Alas it was found that most of
the costly and massive family plate had been previously carried off;
and as it had been sold and melted down at once, none of it could
be recovered. Some of the jewels were discovered by their peculiar
setting, and were found in the possession of pawnbrokers and country
people, totally ignorant of their great value, but others could not be
traced.

The search for the will continued with the aid of careful workmen,
under the supervision of Edward himself. But again and again he
was disappointed and baffled. The oak panels of each room had been
carefully removed, but no secret drawer could be discovered.

Meanwhile, rumours began to reach Edward of certain Macdonalds who
were about to dispute his right to act, and he was daily harassed by
letters from distant relatives of the late laird, each claiming to be
heir-at-law. He therefore fully expected the case would ere long be
brought before some court.


One day, as he was seated buried in desponding cogitations, a
reminiscence of his childhood flashed into his mind. He recollected
his mother's curious ancient cabinet of inlaid wood and silver, and
how one day, as she was cleaning and putting it to rights, she by
accident saw what appeared to be merely a tiny worm-eaten hole. Into
this she inserted a fine knitting-needle, and started with surprise
when a small drawer shot out, containing some old family documents and
a few rare coins. This incident had faded from his mind until present
circumstances revived it.

He rose, determined not to summon the workmen again; he would go alone
and recommence the search. Of course, as is generally the case, they
had hunted through all the likely places first, and Ethel's little
sanctum was the last to be invaded. This also was panelled in oak.
In several of the other rooms, two or three sliding panels had been
detected by the accidental touching of springs. But the secret drawer
which Mr. Macdonald had mentioned could not be found. Ethel's room, it
was argued, being in such a remote situation, would certainly contain
nothing valuable. The search there had consequently been less careful.

"Here is a sliding panel!" cried Edward, with delight, as he stood in
a dark corner on one side of the bookcase, in the room which he had
entered on the night when he appeared as an unbidden guest.

On examining it by the aid of a taper, he observed a little hole in the
back. But he had nothing at hand small enough to insert in it. Suddenly
he recollected Ethel's workbox, turned to it, and lifted the lid. There
lay a slender stiletto, covered with rust, in its silken band. Would it
serve his purpose? He drew it out, and inserted the point in the hole,
when out sprang a secret drawer, which contained the "Will of Alexander
Macdonald, of Muirstane, Esquire," giving the estate to his niece for
life, and after her death to her eldest surviving child.

Who can describe Edward's feelings, as he fell on his knees to utter a
fervent "Thank God!"

With this in his possession, he could hold the estate while he searched
for the wife and child to whom it belonged. But before he went out
into the world to find them, one task yet remained to him. He unlocked
the old man's desk, and took out the bundle of letters that had never
reached their destination. His passionate rage boiled up again as he
read his own letters from America, full of loving, tender words, which
had not even been seen by the dear eyes that had looked up so wistfully
into his, the day he had said "Farewell." Those words would have poured
balm into the wounded lonely heart, and filled it with patient waiting
for his return.

"He murdered her!" said he bitterly.

Then recollecting his words of forgiveness uttered beside the dying
bed, he checked the further outburst of wrath. There were only two
or three of Ethel's letters, and these were directed to the shipping
office in Liverpool whither he had told her to write first. They
wrung his heart as he read them, being full of tender longings for
his return, even ere he had left England. There were hopes and fears
concerning the future, and entreaties that he would, if possible, come
back before their child should be born. Then followed a heart-rending
account of her lonely life. But all this was before she had returned to
Muirstane House, so they told nothing of her miseries there.

"I must go and look for Nannie, soon," she had written in a postscript
attached to the last letter.

At first Edward could not think to whom she referred. But it gradually
dawned upon his recollection that this must be the faithful old nurse,
Nannie McEwen, who had come with her to Muirstane, and stayed until she
was grown-up. He had never seen her, because, owing to circumstances
in her own family, she had been obliged to leave her situation before
he came to Scotland. This woman was now the missing link in his chain
of evidence; he must set out to find her. But to obtain any news of
her was a difficult matter, as in the lapse of years there had been
considerable alterations at Muirstane. The post-office had changed
hands, and though several of the inhabitants could tell him about
Nannie's departure, no one could recollect whither she had gone.

At last one old man said, "She used to tell o' her brither at Portree,
in Skye; didna she gang to him?"


This dubious information, scanty as it was, was all Edward had to act
upon. But he lost no time in setting out on his journey to this distant
place. All necessary directions, together with the management of the
estate, were left in the hands of his factor, and a respectable man and
his wife were placed in charge of the house.

The railway only took him a comparatively small part of the way, but he
was not sorry to exchange it for a seat on the top of a coach, which
started early one September morning. The country through which it took
him was wild and beautiful, and the autumnal tints on the foliage were
red and rare. On, on he went all day, over brown moorlands, past lonely
tarns, up and down steep braes, until the sun sank to rest behind
the purple mountains, and still a fresh set of horses was needed for
yet another stage. It was midnight when the coach finally stopped at
a ferry, where most of the travellers turned in thankfully to sleep
at the little wayside inn close at hand. Not so Edward Pryse; he had
neither time nor inclination to loiter on his way with such an end in
view.

A boat was rocking on the water, and he, with some others, stepped into
it. It was scarcely discernible by the pale light of stars. The persons
who seated themselves formed a picturesque group. Two Highland lassies
were together, and Edward had helped a lame beggar in, he was followed
by the bearer of the mail-bags. One of the boatmen held a bull's-eye
lantern, whose red light flashed brightly on the figures as they rowed
through the mysterious and shadowy darkness. The girls began singing
Jacobite songs, much to the delight of the men, who cheered heartily,
and Edward enjoyed the plaintive sound of the voices, combined with
the gentle movement of the gliding boat. They were soon across, and
after the party had scrambled up the slimy, slippery landing-place, the
boatman came to demand his fares.

"I canna pay ye," said the mendicant. "Ye'll surely tak' me for
naethin'; I'm ganging to the sacrament at Murthlie."

Edward heard the whining tones of remonstrance, and thrust some coppers
into the outstretched hand, which were received with a burst of
grateful thanks.

"How soon can I go on?" he asked at the inn, to which a few footsteps
brought him.

"Ye maun either gang the nicht in her Majesty's mail-cart, or bide till
to-morrow nicht the same hour," replied the woman, who had come out to
greet him.

Edward looked up at the twinkling stars. "It is a fine night," he
thought; "by this time to-morrow there may be pouring rain."

"I will continue my journey now," he said aloud, as he followed the
woman.

After hastily swallowing some slight refreshment which she quickly set
before him, he took his seat behind the driver of the primitive and
clumsy looking mail-cart, with one other passenger, a girl, and the
rough vehicle presently jolted off. The driver had a boon companion by
his side, and the two kept up a ceaseless guttural chatter in Gaelic,
not one word of which Edward could understand. The girl was inclined to
be friendly and communicative, but, finding her companion taciturn and
absent, she soon relapsed into silence.

They travelled on in this way for about two hours, when they reached a
lonely bit of moorland with the lights of a village glimmering faintly
in the distance, and will-o'-the-wisps flitting over the fens, as if to
beguile the unwary traveller. The girl jumped down.

"There is my home," she said to Edward, pointing in the direction of
the lights.

The driver remarked something to her in Gaelic as she handed him his
fare, and laughing her reply, she ran fearlessly off into what seemed
to Edward a lonely, hopeless path.

"She is certain to find what she seeks," thought he; "as for me, I may
be chasing a will-o'-the-wisp."

Edward Pryse never forgot that journey; its silence and isolation
pressed upon his spirit, and everything that came into his mind took a
sombre tinge of melancholy. The long white roads seemed endless, and he
grew numb and chilled, waiting at the doors of wayside public-houses,
into which the driver and his friend disappeared to indulge in
potations which would have rendered driving a perilous risk if the
horses had not known their way.

Edward was thankful to warm himself by trudging up the long hills,
pausing every now and then to listen to the impetuous rushing of
mountain streams he could not see. He watched through that mysterious
hour when night and dawn meet, when the transition from darkness to
light is so gradual that it is scarcely perceptible. By degrees the
faint green tinge in the sky melted into lemon, then red and purple
streaks were painted in bold dashes of colour, and all the glorious
pomp that ushers in the king of day came up in bright triumph, and
spread across the heavens. The great Cuchullin Mountains that in the
dim early twilight had loomed out in giant obscurity, were now bathed
in morning blue, and stood out grand and glowing in a shimmering haze.
An awe crept over Edward's spirit as he gazed.

"How little," thought he, "appear the joys and sorrows of human life,
when looking at those eternal hills that stand enfolded with the
majesty of God."

And then, almost unconsciously, his heart rose higher than the highest
of those mighty peaks, to God Himself. The grand old words of the
prayer of Moses came back to his remembrance:

   "'Lord, Thou hast been our dwelling-place in all generations. Before
the mountains were brought forth, or ever Thou hadst formed the earth
and the world, even from everlasting to everlasting, Thou art God.'"

The earthly radiance represented, though but faintly, the spiritual
day-dawn, which with its everlasting light doth touch the awakened
soul, bidding it reflect the heavenly glory, and stand in God's eternal
sunshine for evermore.

Edward's meditations were suddenly interrupted by the driver informing
him that Portree was in sight. And after a few more miles of level
road, the mail-cart rattled into the sleeping town, for it was still
early, and few of the inhabitants were astir.

A good breakfast and comfortable bed were not unwelcome to our
exhausted traveller, who, after recruiting worn-out nature for a few
hours, rose in eager haste to seek Nannie McEwen.

The place was certainly not an inviting one, for the huts of the very
poor people were little better than pigsties, full of peat-reek and
unpleasant odours. The women looked prematurely old, and sunken low in
the scale of civilisation, as they trudged past him, carrying on their
backs the stacks of peat they had been cutting from the bogs.

Edward spoke to several of them, but a shake of the head, or an answer
in Gaelic, was all he received in return. There were many in the town
itself that spoke English, but they stared with surprise on hearing
Nannie McEwen inquired for. At last, after a fruitless wandering of
more than an hour, he returned to the hotel, and consulted with the
landlord.

"Maybe Janet kens wha he means," quoth he.

And a pleasant elderly woman was summoned.

"Nannie McEwen!" she said thoughtfully, as if hunting through the
storehouse of her memory. "Ay, I ken fine wha ye're seeking. She cam'
frae Muirstane auchteen years syne, to nurse her sick brither. I mind
it weel, for it was sune after my Jeannie was born, and she cam' to
help me through my trouble when I had nane but hersel' to be wi' me."

"And where is she now?" asked Edward, breathing shortly.

"'Deed, that I dinna ken. Her brither dee'd a year after she cam' to
Portree, an' after the burial, she gaed South, but whaur tae, nane kens
but Him that's above. She lived in yon wee hoose," she added, pointing
with her hand; "but they're a' fremd fowk that bides there noo."

Edward's heart sank to the depths; no more questions would come to his
lips, and he turned away a hopeless, desolate man. The feeble clue had
snapped, and he was as much in the dark as ever.

He strode on fiercely, past the outskirts of the town, not caring
whither his footsteps led him, and wandered lonely for miles along
bare, brown tracts of moorland, feeling the sterile scenery was in
keeping with his dashed hopes. The light that had glimmered like a
guiding-star upon his dark, uncertain way had proved a false meteor,
luring him on but to mock him at last.


Nothing remained for him to do now, but to retrace his way to the drear
and desolate abode at Muirstane. Time was now no object to him, for
there was nought to be gained by extra speed; the wheels were off his
chariot, and he drave it heavily. His bitter disappointment destroyed
all the self-confidence and certainty of success with which he had
started. He could only go on living over and over again the sad, sad
past, from the very beginning to the bitter end.

How bright and careless were the happy days of childhood! Those
butterfly chases across the dappled meadows, with his merry little
sister, the child Minnie of long ago; their schemes and plans laid
together; their mischievous pranks and the punishments that followed,
and Minnie's anguish when the sentence of chastisement fell on him.
He could see her now, standing in one corner of the study, with her
fingers in her ears and her pinafore over her fair curls, screaming
with all her might, so as not to hear the sounds attendant on the
whipping he was led off to receive. And since then, she had gone to
sleep, so early, oh, so early, under the hushing sycamore trees.

The love of his sister Silexa too, how deep and true it was, shown so
often in pleadings and entreaties for the youthful culprits! Edward
recalled the countless times she had screened them at her own expense
from their father's anger. Indeed, he could never remember anything
but the most passionate devotedness to himself on the part of both his
sisters. He saw now how it had ministered to his selfishness. Silexa
would have sacrificed anything she possessed to this daring, handsome
brother, and thought nothing too good or costly, if only it would
gratify him. This was the one weak point in that strong woman's nature.
She would have given her life for Edward.

He now recalled with sorrow that morning in his sunny youth when he
had bidden a joyful farewell to school life and his old home at the
Rectory, to begin what he believed would be a bright independent career
in the great city of London. Minnie's tears and caresses had made his
heart ache a little on saying good-bye, and so had Silexa's dry earnest
eyes, so full of yearning love and anxiety as she thrust a purse of
her own making into his hands, filled with some months' savings of her
scanty allowance. He never knew of what self-denial that little sum was
proof.

He had chafed impatiently at the words of counsel and advice, given
somewhat sternly by his father at the gate, not being able to discern
the high sense of honour and the parental love from which they
sprung. He was free now to be his own master; and with that feeling
predominant, the bold, blithe boy had gone out into life, determined to
enjoy himself at any cost.

For a year all went prosperously. Mr. Lyndwood, his father's oldest
friend, was prepared to love him as a son, and consequently allowed him
many indulgences which as a stranger he would not have had. Alas, for
Edward! He lacked the foundation of all prosperity—

   "'The fear of the Lord, which is the beginning of wisdom...'"

And therefore, when temptation presented itself, he turned aside and
fell in to the snares of the Evil One, who took him captive at his will.

Bad companions first led him astray, and though Edward shrunk from
some of their ways, remembering his upright father and his pure-minded
sisters at home, he by degrees was drawn into a vortex, the slope
towards which begins with a little downhill which is very gradual.
Unknown to his friend and protector, he frequented gambling-houses,
and there betted heavily, his first winnings encouraging him to go on
recklessly.

At last, the crash came; he owed two hundred pounds, which was to be
paid at once. He dared not write to his father, and he knew Silexa had
no money at her command. Mr. Lyndwood would probably turn him from
his office, and expose his disgrace to the whole world. There was
but one course open to him, so thought he in his blinded folly. This
was, to take the money from the office, where he had the handling of
considerable sums; he thought he could pay it back by instalments.
It was a secret impossible to keep long; he got an inkling that the
accounts were about to be looked into, so, having just received his
salary, he absconded suddenly.

Mr. Lyndwood, irrespective of the loss of the money, felt so keenly for
Dr. Pryse, that he positively refused to make the matter public either
by instituting a search for Edward or letting any one beyond his own
office know of it. He believed the wild youth knew him well enough to
be assured of his forgiveness for his father's sake, should he confess
his guilt bravely and honestly. Therefore, after writing to his old
friend at St. Elvis, he declined to move further in the matter.

We are already cognizant of the effect this dire news had at the
Rectory, but only Edward knew of a visit to his home in disguise. After
having waited in vain for an answer to his advertisement, he came down
to St. Elvis, and under cover of darkness crept round to the study
window and peeped in. The window was open, and while standing under
shelter of the thick ivy, he heard Silexa pleading for the outcast, and
then what seemed to the youth his father's cruel, bitter words. He was
to be disowned as a son until he had shown his repentance by repaying
the money, and his sisters were forbidden to hold any communication
with the offender. Edward crept away again; rage, hatred, and pride
struggling in his heart.

"Yet why should I care," he argued, "when he has treated me so
shamefully? I hope I may never see his face again."

Ah, if Silexa had but known who was outside under the stars that night,
things might perhaps have turned out differently.

As it was, Edward quitted the home of his childhood that same hour,
and after travelling day and night finally set foot on board a vessel
bound for America. On landing there, his way was prosperous, for he
had invented a most plausible story to satisfy the friends he had made
during the voyage, several of whom were most anxious to do their utmost
for the handsome young man. He had a marvellously winning address and
manner, which went far to gain him supporters, so that when he had
succeeded in quieting his conscience, he congratulated himself on his
so-called lucky hits.

He obtained without much difficulty a lucrative post in a good office,
and ended by being employed by a large and well-known American firm.
After working steadily and honourably for some years, he was by degrees
advanced to a position of great trust, and received a high salary.

To do Edward Pryse justice, he did not forget that some day he ought to
restore the two hundred pounds he had stolen. Indeed, his pride could
not brook the idea of being branded as a thief for life. But his dull
and blunted conscience could not show him his sin in its true light, as
a guilty offence towards God.

"If once," he argued, "the amount were actually paid, no blame ought to
attach to me."

But he did not think it necessary meantime to deny himself anything
that contributed towards his enjoyment of life. He must have everything
he wanted just the same, and some day, when he was a rich man, and
could do it without discomfort to himself, he would discharge the debt.

One day, he was sent for by his employers, who informed him they
wished him to undertake a journey to Scotland on behalf of the firm.
He assented willingly, and was then told that his errand was to a
certain Alexander Macdonald, Esquire, of Muirstane, who had a large
sum of money invested in the business. There were some affairs to be
transacted with him which could only be entrusted to a confidential
person, able to undertake the commission.

In fulfilment of this errand, a handsome stranger suddenly appeared
at Muirstane, to the no small astonishment and curiosity of its
inhabitants, who watched him making his way up to the big house, where
he was at once received as a guest.

The remainder of Edward's story may be easily conjectured. He was
detained on this business several months, and during that period he
made the most of his time. He had not been many days a guest in the
spacious mansion before he discovered he had fallen hopelessly in love
with the old man's niece, Ethel Macdonald, the most beautiful girl he
had ever seen. The attachment was mutual, for Ethel, nothing loth, gave
her heart quickly away to this noble-looking and fascinating man.

Mr. Macdonald never dreamt of such a possibility, though the young
people were necessarily much thrown together, for Ethel was in his mind
as much disposed of as if the marriage he wished for her had already
taken place. A wealthy suitor had come forward to win the hand of the
beautiful girl, and it mattered little to the hard old man that she had
openly expressed her great dislike to this proposal.

Alexander Macdonald had an iron will, which few dared to dispute. He
was consequently both a tyrant and a coward. His anger was great,
therefore, when one morning, about a month after Edward's arrival, the
young man formally asked his consent to make Ethel his bride. A direct
refusal was of course the answer. And it was politely hinted to the
guest that it would be desirable for him to take up his quarters in the
village during the remainder of his stay—a request acceded to at once.

But Edward's will was strong also. Like a spoiled child, he felt
that what he wanted he must have at any cost. And without pausing
to consider the consequences of his hasty and selfish purpose, he
persuaded Ethel, during her uncle's absence of a week from home, to
consent to their marriage before his return. They were accordingly
united in the presence of witnesses, who heard them solemnly pronounced
to be man and wife.

Excuse must be made for the lonely orphan, who had been brought up
without a mother's care, and whose life had hitherto been so loveless
and forlorn. Whatever her uncle's feelings were towards her, he never
outwardly had manifested any fondness for her, and she naturally turned
from him to rest in one whom she deemed in every way worthy of her
heart's best affections. She had yet to learn by fierce and adverse
winds that her trust must be placed far higher than in mortal man. The
sharp storm drove her to seek refuge in the cleft of the Rock, and
"through much tribulation" to "enter into the kingdom of God."

Edward's conduct was, on the contrary, most culpable and selfish, for
he knew that in four months he must depart again for America alone. It
did not suit his plans to take Ethel with him: she must therefore be
left behind to carry on the battle of life single-handed. His purpose
was to leave her at Muirstane, and return to America to wind up his
connection with the firm; this he thought would not take him long
to accomplish. He hoped then to return, and find some employment in
England which would enable him to settle down with his wife there.

The terrible fear of losing Ethel before he could come back would not
allow him to defer the marriage, for she had told him of her uncle's
designs with regard to the rich and detested man he was bent on her
accepting. The thought of another winning his darling was too abhorrent
to be tolerated for an instant. It was this that silenced the voice of
his conscience. As soon as she was lawfully his own, he brought his
wife to the small cottage he had taken in Muirstane, where he intended
to leave her during his absence.



CHAPTER XIV

NANNIE'S HOME

MR. MACDONALD'S anger was of a most dangerous kind, when on his return,
he learned from the servants what had taken place. He did not fly into
a furious passion, or grow livid with rage, but he swore secretly to
have his revenge to the very utmost. Ethel should rue the day she
married Edward Pryse, and her husband should be hers no longer from
the time he left the shores of Scotland. There in his miser's den, he
laid the deep schemes which were to be so fatally carried out. But at
present, he could not afford to be uncivil to Edward, because there
were matters still to be arranged, in which it would be as well to
propitiate this friend of the firm. So, though cold, he was outwardly
courteous to him when they had occasion to meet. But with quiet
malignity watched for the opportunity of crushing his victims.

Ethel seldom went near the great house during the remainder of her
husband's stay. She was afraid to meet her uncle alone, and she felt
perfectly safe and happy with her heart's love. How peacefully those
summer days glided away, as by degrees she beguiled her husband into
telling her all his past history! As usual, Edward managed to throw a
fictitious gloss over the ugly circumstances of the office robbery,
calling it only borrowing. And though Ethel looked up somewhat
doubtfully with her innocent eyes of truth, he argued so plausibly,
that at last it appeared to her a far less dreadful crime than it
really was.

"But you will send the money back when you get to America, won't you?"
she pleaded.

Edward promised again and again.

She then continued: "You will take me to your own home some day? For,
of course, when the debt is paid, your father will not be angry any
more. You will be forgiven, and he will receive us with open arms. I
should be frightened to go there all alone, but with you, it would be
different. Silexa and Minnie will love me as a sister, and we shall all
be so happy together."

"Yes," said Edward; "we will go to St. Elvis."

For the lapse of years had softened his bitter feelings towards his
father, and he longed to see the old home once more. He described to
his young wife his boyish haunts, and all the fun he and Minnie used
to have with good Roger Rudd, the hale and hearty fisherman, who never
refused to take them out in his boat with his daughter Lettice, the
quiet, sober maiden, who was somewhat too demure for the mischievous
pair. Ethel liked hearing everything Edward could tell her about his
sisters, and already felt as if she belonged to them.

"Our baby, if it is a girl, must be called after them," she said one
evening, just before he started. "You wish it, don't you? Ermyn is such
a lovely name; it must be first, I think, and then Silexa."

"As you please, darling. Yes, it will make them love the little thing
all the more when they see it. My earliest recollections are associated
with the name of Ermyn."

And then he told her of the dear old church, and the ancient monument
with the dirty little marble angels.

But the parting was a sore and bitter one, as he clasped a beautiful
locket set in pearls round her neck, containing two curls of hair, and
the monogram of double E across it. How Ethel clung to him and wept, as
he held her to his breast, and comforted her with promises. He really
would come back as soon as he could, and it might be much sooner than
she thought.

Edward, in his selfishness, little dreamed, as he was going forth
once more into the busy whirl of life, of the cruel weight of utter
loneliness and desolation that sunk upon the poor wife's heart, as she
watched him disappear from her wistful gaze.

Edward went over these scenes again and again, as now, in an agony of
despair, he flung himself upon the ground, and shed bitter tears for
his lovely lost one. They were tears also of heartfelt contrition, for
he could now see that all these griefs were but the consequences of his
own sin.

As he lay there on the dreary turf bog, with a white mist wrapping him
round, he cried aloud from the depths of his bursting heart:

   "'I have gone astray like a lost sheep.'

   "'Against Thee, Thee only have I sinned, and done this evil in Thy
sight.'"

After a while, this was followed by the other cry:

   "'I will arise and go to my Father, and will say unto Him, Father,
I have sinned against heaven, and in Thy sight, and am no more worthy to
be called Thy son.'"

How the long winter passed he could not tell. It seemed like untold
ages as he went about Muirstane, lonely and miserable, day by day,
trying in vain to catch at the faintest shadow that might indicate
the spot in the wide lonely world where he could begin to track the
footsteps of his beloved. If she were not in the fated ship, he might
yet possibly find her. But the remembrance of that beautiful child's
strong resemblance to Ethel forbade him to cling much to this hope. Yet
why would Ethel be in a ship bound for Ireland? There was the mystery!

On the other hand, if it was Ethel who was drowned (and he had small
doubt of this in his own mind), what proof could he give to Mrs. Gwynne
that Ermyn was his child? He had no evidence of the fact to bring
forward, and he could not claim her without it. Night and day he prayed
for light in this darkness; but he had to wait God's time for it. He
dragged through the months of frost and snow somehow, doing his best
to beguile the time by relieving the wants of the tenantry around, who
blessed the day Mr. Pryse had come among them as their landlord. His
thoughtful kindness for them made him many friends, who were all on the
look-out to glean tidings that might help him in his search; for all
had loved and pitied his gentle wife.

Spring had clothed the hills and trees with living verdure. But as the
green deepened and spread on every hand, there was no resurrection hope
and gladness in the heart of the lonely man. Summer was at hand, and
what then?

He was seated, one sultry day some weeks later, in the little general
shop, talking to the mistress of it about some repairs she wanted done,
when a merry-looking Scotch lassie stepped across the threshold.

"Ye'll no' ken me," she said, smiling to the shop-woman. "I'm a
stranger here; but maybe ye'll mind Nannie McEwen, wha bided here afore
I was born."

"Ay, ay, my bonnie lassie, I mind her weel," said the woman.

While Edward started to his feet, too much staggered at the name to
speak a word.

"I'm on my road to my aunt's," continued the girl, "wha bides at
Castletoun, and my grandmother, Mistress McEwen, bade me come by
Muirstane, and see gin I could hear ony word o' her bonnie leddy, wha
lived here afore she was married."

"Where does your grandmother live, my lassie?" said Edward, pale and
trembling.

"Eh, sir, it's a lang, lang piece frae Muirstane; I've been three days
on the road."

She then proceeded to detail her route by coach and ferry, from this
out of the world habitation among the hills.

"It's a wee bit town," she added, "but when ye cross the ferry at
'Glenairg,' onybody 'll tell ye whaur Nannie McEwen bides."

The girl stared with amazement at her interlocutor, wondering why he
questioned her so closely—he, a stranger she had never set eyes on
before. But she did not object to give him as much information as he
liked, especially when, on quitting the shop, he slipped a bright piece
of gold into her hand.

"At last! At last!" he panted to himself, as he went up the hill to his
lonely dwelling. "O God, I thank Thee, Thou hast heard my cry!"

To cram a few necessaries into a bag, and depart in joyful haste, was
his work that afternoon. What mattered difficulties and fatigues,
however great they might be? The wings of Hope had grown again, and
she was now soaring on before him as the dove of peace, with the
olive-branch in her mouth! He was on the right track at last!

It was a tedious and difficult journey, but Edward never thought of
that, as, weary and thankful, he stepped into the ferry-boat that would
land him within sight of Nannie's home. The man who rowed him across
had never received such a lavish guerdon before. He invoked a blessing
on the head of the traveller, who sprang lightly out, and began
speeding away up the hill opposite, as if he were only setting out on
his journey.

A little mountain lake lay cradled in the bosom of the hills, and on
its peaceful breast slept soft lights and purple shadows. The loftiest
peak blushed rosy red in the sunset glow, and was reflected in the
clear water, making the dark, green depths on the shady side seem
deeper still. A well-to-do looking farmhouse was situated on a bit
of rising ground just above the lake, and its numerous outbuildings
and byres sloping towards the shore gave it a prosperous look. Rude
flagstone steps inserted here and there among the heather led down
from the door to the water, and a girl in a gay kirtle was on her way
thither to fill the pail she was carrying. An aged woman stood knitting
at the door, every now and then looking up at the grey and pink clouds
above her head.

As Edward drew nearer, he had time to observe her dress and appearance.
She wore a dark stuff gown, which showed a clear white handkerchief
folded across under the rather open bodice, and on her head a spotless
mutch trimmed with narrow lace, having a black ribbon bound above it.
Some grey locks strayed over the wrinkled forehead, and the soft brown
eyes had a kindly light in them. A sweet smile of welcome played about
her mouth as the stranger advanced. Edward could understand, when he
looked at her, why Ethel had fled to her motherly arms for refuge.

"A braw nicht, sir," she said, dropping a curtsey.

Edward courteously returned her greeting, and put the eager question:
"Does Nannie McEwen live here?"

"'Deed, ay, sir, I am Nannie McEwen mysel', though I dinna ken wha I'm
speakin' till. Come ye ben the hoose, sir," she added, leading the way.
"It's by-ordinar' cauld the nicht."

The old woman turned to look at her guest, and great was her surprise
to see he had sunk down on a seat by the door, deathly pale, and
gasping for breath.

"The Lord preserve us a'! What's wrang wi' the gentleman? What ails ye,
sir?"

"Pardon me," he said, struggling back into self-possession; "the long
journey has wearied me. Thank God, I have found you at last. I am Ethel
Macdonald's husband, Edward Pryse."

Nannie's knitting fell from her fingers, as with upraised hands she
said, "What! Him that crossed the seas, and left her lane. Ye dinna
mean to tell me ye are my bonnie leddy's ain gude man! Wae's me! Wae's
me!" And, sitting down, she rocked herself to and fro, and began to
weep bitterly.

"Did she come to you from Muirstane?" asked Edward, white and trembling.

"Ay, that did she, puir lammie! My mitherless bairn cam' to me ae mirk
nicht a' droukit wi' the rain, spent wi' the journey, and maist deed
wi' the cauld. Whaur is she, sir, the noo? Oh, gin I kent that!"

"I believe she is with God," replied Edward, with a groan, "but tell me
all you know."

Then, as he grew calmer, he gave the history of his arrival at
Muirstane, and all the awful facts his visit there brought to light,
the long fruitless search, the chance meeting with Nannie's grandchild,
and his subsequent journey hither.

"Puir, puir lad! It's a sair heart ye'll carry aboot a' your days,
for I dinna think ye'll meet again till ye meet in oor Father's hoose
above. It's an ower true tale, as I ken fu' weel, though I didna think
to hae tauld it to her ain gude man. I'll tell ye a' aboot it, sir, and
ye'll just bide the nicht wi' us. The bairns and my son are awa', but
I'll do my best to mak' ye comfortable."

Edward thanked her with a sad look of gratitude in his weary eyes,
entreating her to proceed.

"It's fourteen years," continued Nannie, "since she cam' to my door.
Weel do I mind hoo she fell greetin' aboot my neck, an' I clappit her
an' made o' her same as she had been the bit wean I mind her in her ain
nursery lang, lang syne. Eh, for the sad tale she had to tell. It'll
be a lang reckonin' her uncle wull hae to gie to the Almighty; and
little did I think to live to see the day when my bonnie leddy wad walk
barefit an' hungry. We put her till her bed, an' twa days after that,
her bonnie bairn was upon her breast."

"A girl?" eagerly interrupted Edward.

"Ay, a fine bit lassie; I was the first that handled her, and her
mither's face was like a simmer's day when I laid the wean in her
arms. But the time gaed by, and nae word cam' till her frae across the
sea. Mony's the day my heart was wae for her! Ye may weel believe we
was prood to hae her under oor roof. But it was aye a sair thocht to
her that she an' the bairn were burdensome to us. My ain dochter, our
bairns' mither, was ta'en awa' before she cam' to us, but the bairnies
had a sair work wi' the wee leddy.

"Mony a time my dear bairn wad say she maun gang to Liverpool, and
speir for news hersel' amang the shipowners, but that wasna wark for
the like o' her, an' ne'er a fute wad we let her gang near it. She wad
traik in an' oot wi' the bairn, for she was a' the pleasure she had.
But there came ae day, an' wae's me that I hae lived to tell it (it
was when the bairnie was aboot saxteen months auld); I had gane to the
town, an' when I cam' back, she was awa', nane could tell me whaur.
Mony a weary mile I followed her, and mony a ane I speired at gin they
had seen her, but frae that day till noo I've never looked on her sweet
face, nor heard tell o' her, or o the wean. Eh, man, it wad be a blithe
day for me gin ye had brocht me word frae my mitherless bairn."

Edward's head sank lower and lower upon his breast as he listened, and
the kind-hearted old woman would fain have comforted him as her own son.

"Whiles in the gloamin' I'll still gang doon to the burnside, and I'll
maist think I'll fin' them there, where mony and mony a day I've seen
them sitting amang the gowans, upon the grass, or heard her singing to
the bairn wi' a greet in her voice; or the bairnie crowin' over the
brave locket o' gowd and pearls she wad hing aboot its neck."

"The locket!" cried Edward, starting up. "Where is it? Oh that I could
see it! I had forgotten about it."

"She took it wi' her," said Nannie; "but I'll show ye the buik she
wrote in ilka day. I'm thinkin' she'd forgotten it when she gaed awa'.
I'm no scholard, sir, and canna read write; but nae doot ye'll mak' it
oot."

Edward snatched the volume from her hand, and with trembling fingers
opened it to read on the first page:

   "'My Diary, since the sad day my beloved Edward left me.'"

Late, late into the night he sat up perusing those precious pages,
though Nannie entreated him to take his rest. A sorrowful memorial it
was, written from the depths of a breaking heart, but a heart that
was true and loyal to him to the very end. All through those dreary
months, when every outward evidence tended to make her believe she was
forgotten and forsaken by her husband, nothing had shaken her firm
belief in his love.

These pages supplied all the missing links, so ruthlessly broken and
scattered by wicked malice. They told of her cruel and bitter bondage
in her uncle's house, of his sneers and taunts concerning her marriage,
of his villainous hints about desertion and forgetfulness, and of her
mad desperation, when at last, in spite of the watchful guard set over
her, she determined to fly from it all to find her faithful Nannie. She
recounted her long and difficult journey to the house of her old nurse,
and all the never-to-be-forgotten kindness showered upon her there from
first to last.

The faint writing went on to record her baby's birth, and the untold
joy with which she first held her darling Edward's child in her arms;
the baptism of her precious infant by the aged Scotch minister in the
wee kirk on the hill, and all the little loving details that only a
mother's pen can indite.

   "I have called her after our two dear sisters, as my Edward
wished—Ermyn Silexa," so ran words. "Some day they will hold her in
their arms and love her dearly. Oh, when Edward comes to take me to
them, all will be well."

There was a break in the narrative; then came this despairing entry:

   "Oh, he must be dead! The letter telling him of my arrival here and
of our child's birth has come back to me. When will this night of
sorrow end? But my Lord and Saviour is with me to cheer its gloom. His
voice hath spoken to me. 'Himself hath done it.' I shall go softly all
my years in the bitterness of my soul. 'In the world ye shall have
tribulation; but be of good cheer, I have overcome the world.' My tired
head is pillowed on His breast; the storm cannot harm me there. Oh, my
Edward! My husband! My one and only love; we shall meet again!"

Here the manuscript broke off abruptly. There was no hint whatever
given as to her intended flight.

Nannie believed she fully meant to return, as she left several things
behind her, and would never have taken her final departure without a
single good-bye.

Edward could only guess whither his wife went by her mention of
Liverpool in her diary, as the place where she hoped to learn tidings
of him. To Liverpool therefore he must go next. And as he mentally
arranged his journey, he thought of the last time he was there, when he
had arrived from America a heart-broken and desolate widower.

Strange faces had looked at him on the busy, bustling quay, some with
a glance of pity, others cynical and scornful. Now he seemed to have
passed through a hideous dream, only to awake to find the reality even
sadder. He recalled that last tender parting, and the pressure of those
soft arms about his neck. Oh, how his heart ached as the words of the
poet haunted him:

   "I know not what I said; what she replied
    Lives like eternal sunshine in my heart:
    And then I murmured, 'Oh! we never part
      My love, my life, my bride.'

   "Oft as I wake at morn, I seem to see
    A moment, the sweet shadow of that shade,
    Her blessed face, as it were loth to fade,
      Turned back to look on me."



CHAPTER XV

DAVID AND EUDORA

THE summer sunshine was glowing fervently in the green Welsh valley,
its sleepy stillness being scarcely broken by a sound. The dusky
long-horned cattle strayed dreamily along to stand in a cool swamp near
the stream, or sought the shadow of bits of projecting rock, as they
whisked off swarms of persistent flies with their tails. The murmur of
bees was heard in one long hum through the flowers, and a cloudless
blue sky canopied over all. Even the sea had a slumbrous sound, as the
sparkling ripples ran up in succession over the shining sand, for there
was no wind to wake them into mirth.

The ivy-grown Rectory was still and quiet, with every blind drawn-down
to keep the sunbeams from the shabby carpets and curtains. This was
one of Silexa's careful habits, and she often sighed to find that in
spite of her vigilance, both colour and pattern had somehow contrived
to escape. Voices and clatter were combined as usual at the back door,
where the daily business went on in the old accustomed round. But in
front, the flowers in the small circular beds on the lawn seemed to
have it all to themselves. But no, surely the thick branches of the
weeping willow stirred, as a sound of merry laughter came from beneath
its leaves.

"It is much too hot in here," cried Eudora; "this close green keeps
away all the air from us."

"I thought you would find it so," laughed Ermyn, "but we need not stay
here. Let us go across to the garden opposite. There are so many shady
corners there."

"Yes, so we will," replied Eudora, "but let me run in first and fetch
my hat. The shadows will soon lengthen now, and when it gets cooler, we
will walk towards the sea."

"How bright and strong she has grown!" thought Ermyn, as she watched
the lithe figure step across the grass. "Her hair looks as if sunbeams
were at play in it. Oh, how glad I am to have my darling back again!"

The two girls formed a striking contrast, as, with their arms linked
together, they passed through the Rectory gate and crossed the
road—Ermyn's hair shining glossy and dark against Eudora's soft, golden
locks. The blue eyes and delicate complexion of one fair face setting
off the rich, rare beauty of the other.

The enclosure they entered was a good specimen of a thoroughly
old-fashioned garden, or rather gardens, for there were four or five
of them leading out of one another by small doors. In the first were
mostly flowers—roses, sweet-peas, mignonette, hollyhocks, geraniums,
clove carnations, nasturtiums, and scabius. It was too hot to linger
here, so Eudora led the way down a step into the next division. It was
a well-stocked place, but instead of trim neatness there was luxuriant
confusion. A dear old rural garden, telling of plenty and contentment,
as one sat in its quiet nooks, far-away from the hurry and haste of
noisy, panting cities.

"Let us climb up on this medlar bough," cried Ermyn, as, springing
lightly from the ground, a canopy of leaves spread green above her.

"Is there room for two?" said Eudora. "Ah yes, there is a dear little
niche close to the trunk; that is delightful! Now, Ermyn, I am quite
sure you have a great deal more to tell me."

For it seemed to Eudora that hitherto she had had all the talk. There
had been so much to relate, the friends having been separated for a
longer period than was at first anticipated when the flitting took
place from the Rectory.

The year that had been such a long and sad one in the cottage by the
creek had brought back with its autumn tints the good old rector
and his daughter, but not Eudora. The change to Southsands had
done such wonders for the delicate girl, that the doctor whom Miss
Pryse consulted there thought it would be a foolish risk to expose
her to another winter in St. Elvis until her health was thoroughly
re-established. Silexa had therefore yielded to the kind entreaties
of Mr. Lyndwood's friends that she might stay with them until she was
quite strong.

So, in the midst of a happy household of healthy, merry young people,
our quiet Eudora had found that good spirits and innocent recreations,
combined with numerous interesting and intellectual pursuits, were
among the good gifts showered down from the bounteous hand of our
loving Father, "who giveth us all things richly to enjoy."

It was a bright page in her life; and she was delighted to go over it
all again with Ermyn, who had pined and longed for her friend, thinking
the spring would never pass. But it did at last, and in the summer
sunshine her darling had come back. They both felt the talks would
never end.

"But, Ermyn, why did you not tell me about David?" whispered Eudora,
burying her head among the thick leaves to hide the blush that deepened
on her cheek.

"We wanted it to be a surprise to you," replied Ermyn, without looking
round. "Davie made us all promise faithfully we would not tell; and you
don't know how very difficult it was to keep the secret in my letters."

"But you did keep it," laughed Eudora, untying her hat and flinging
it on the ground. "I was sitting in the old high pew with auntie that
Sunday, when, just as service began, and I expected as usual to see
grandpapa's white head appear in the reading-desk, whom should I see
but David! I could not believe it at first, for I did not even know he
was at home. How I started when I heard his voice! Auntie gave a little
smile from her corner, and then made herself grave again; while I—oh,
Ermyn, how foolish I was!—I was obliged to bend my head quite low and
pretend to be looking for a book on the cushion, because the tears
would come into my eyes."

"He is such a dear, good brother," said Ermyn; "I am so proud of him,
and I cannot tell you what it is to have him once more in the old home.
Our precious mother has not to work any more now, you know. Davie will
not let her, and she looks so sweet in the nice black silk gown he
brought her. No one has such a dear mother as we have, I am sure."

Eudora sighed, and was silent for a moment before she answered; for her
memory was busy recalling the countless times that Lettice had taken
her on her knee when she was a little child, to stroke the fair head
that nestled to her bosom. "I wish she were my—Oh, Ermyn, look at that
greedy bee! He has got more honey than he can possibly carry."

Ermyn, unsuspectingly, did as she was bid, and then turned to look at
her friend. "You never used to have such beautiful rosy cheeks, Dora. I
am so glad you went to Southsands. Are you tired of sitting here?" she
added, noticing that Eudora's eyes had a far-away look in them.

At this moment David appeared, approaching from a distance. The girls
were silent until he came up to the garden gate.

Mary Llewellyn at the same time approached from the opposite direction.

"I came to look for you," she said to Ermyn. "Mother wants you about
something. Can you come home with me now?"

"Yes," replied Ermyn; "I meant to come this morning at any rate.
Good-bye, Dora; I shall be with you again by and by."

David and Eudora stood for a moment to watch the two figures as they
turned to climb the hill.

"Are you going across now, Miss Eudora?"

"Yes, I am," she answered, smiling, and waited for him to speak next.

He was, however, silent.

And she exclaimed, with a gaiety that seemed forced, "I must get that
lovely cluster of wild roses," suddenly springing towards the hedge,
where a long and beautiful spray waved high in air.

As she did so, a piece of honeysuckle that was fastened in her dress
fell to the ground. David picked it up, and then helped her to gather
the wreath.

Eudora took her hat off, and twisted it in gracefully with the ribbon.

David watched her in silence. "You were very happy at Southsands, were
you not, Miss Eudora?" he said, lingering at the gate.

A bright light came into Eudora's eyes as she answered, "Oh yes, so
happy! My friends were all most kind to me, and we used to do such
pleasant things there. But I often longed to come back to St. Elvis, in
spite of it all."

"You are not tired of us yet, then?"

"Davie! Mr. Gwynne," she added, blushing crimson, "how can you say
anything so unkind? I never want to live away from St. Elvis, never,
never."

"Please do not call me Mr. Gwynne," he pleaded. "Have you forgotten our
games on the beach, and all we used to do together?"

"Oh, but you don't seem a bit like the same; you are such a tall—"
"Gentleman," she was going to say, but checked herself, feeling he had
always deserved that name, for the chivalrous courtesy of his manner
was nothing new. "I could scarcely believe it was you," she stammered,
"when I saw you come into church, and yet I was so glad."

A smile like a sunbeam broke over David's grave, earnest face, as he
heard these words. "You will come out again in the old boat soon, won't
you?" he said, holding the Rectory gate open for her to pass through.

"Yes, I will," she answered, with a half-sad look.

"Good-bye, Miss Eudora."

"Good-bye, David."

Eudora had not missed the bit of honeysuckle, and he did not remind her
of it as he held it in his hand.

"How foolish I am!" he murmured, as, striking down through the meadow
path, he turned towards home. "How dare I ever dream of winning her?
My fair queen-lily, growing so far beyond my reach! I must bury my
love deep down in my heart, and let a crust of hard work grow over it.
She must never know it, for it is quite hopeless. How can I, a poor
curate, and not even of gentle birth, think for a moment of asking her
to share my labour? And yet she is not proud. No, simple and sweet as
a spotless flower. But I am poor," he added bitterly, "and so is she.
God has given me a mother and sister to care for; I must not ask Him
for another gift until He sees fit to bestow it upon me Himself. He has
granted me my heart's desire in placing me among my own people, and to
feed and nourish them faithfully and well shall be my life-work.

   "O my God, grant that I may be one of Thy true under-shepherds,
following in Thy footsteps, to lead the flock beside green pastures and
still waters. My Saviour Christ, let me spend and be spent for Thee!"

No one had known of the sharp struggle in David's heart, through those
weeks of suspense, when Eudora hovered between life and death in
that terrible illness. Ermyn's letters had grown sadder and sadder;
he hardly expected to see the sweet face again. He thought God was
going to call her home. Then had come the joyful tidings of hope. Her
bright springtime was not to end here; he would still see its lovely
blossoming.

Oh, those happy weeks, when he had walked beside her, leading the
donkey so carefully, and fearing every breath of the strong sea-breeze
as it blew upon her in its freshness. David's heart knew then for
the first time in all its force what kind of love his was for Eudora
Meredith, but he had kept it hidden and still. It was too sacred to
show to any one. He would not pain her with what he believed to be a
hopeless affection. He must pray to be made brave and self-forgetful in
his work among the people.

Poor Eudora! The love had come into her heart too, but she could not
speak of it to a human being. Her dear Lettice was David's mother.
She must be utterly silent on this point with her. Aunt Silexa never
seemed to suspect such a thing for a moment, or if she did, she had not
betrayed her thought by look or sign. As for Ermyn, it had not entered
into her mind for a moment that her brother could regard Eudora with
other feelings than she did herself, her friend now, and her playfellow
in past years. The trio would be always unbroken.

"I don't think he cares for me!" sighed Eudora, as she ran quickly
upstairs to her room over the porch, and watched David calmly walking
away towards Porthmynwr.


It was one evening soon after this little episode that Ermyn lured
Eudora away for a ramble along the cliff. For a while they rested at
the Sainted Well, drinking the clear water, and playing with the ferns
that fringed its margin. Ermyn had told Eudora about the visit of the
mysterious stranger to St. Elvis; and they now began to discuss the
matter again, wondering why he had painted the picture, and what his
name was. Their speculations ended in their weaving a most romantic
tale about him, and the innocent girls little guessed how near they
were to the truth as, rising, they continued their stroll.

The coast path wound along most unevenly through the prickly gorse,
making long detours round volcanic-looking hollows, where the land
had gradually given way to the encroachments of the sea, leaving
precipitous sides down the rocky cliffs, from whence at a great height
one could look down and watch the waves break into the caverns below.
It was an unsafe route for strangers, but for Ermyn and Eudora, who
had been accustomed to run along it ever since they were quite little
children, there was no danger.

"The sea will look lovely from Chanter's Seat to-night," said Eudora;
"we must wait and see the moon rise."

"Yes, we can," replied Ermyn; "for Davie promised to come and fetch us."

Eudora's heart felt very light after that, as she climbed down the
bit of sloping rock and gave her hand to her companion. One jump, and
they were both hidden from the public path by an angle in the cliff,
where was a ledge hollowed out in the stone overlooking the whole bay,
and known by the name of "Chanter's Seat." It was so-called from the
peculiarity of its echo, which had the charm of doubling and trebling
the power of the human voice, so that when any one sang there, it was
with a marvellously rich and beautiful effect.

"Oh, what a glorious sunset, Ermyn! Just look at those colours across
the water."

The two friends sat together side by side, watching it in silence. The
blue bay stretched out calm and lovely before them, with rose-colour
and green lights playing across its surface, while beyond, over the
horizon, rose the shadowy cloud-land, whose pink and purple outlines
seemed to belong to an ethereal and glorified shore.

"What a depth the sea is below us!" said Eudora, as they leaned over to
gaze down at the gorges, clefts, and chasms into which the water rose
and roared, ascending in fountains of snowy surf, and retiring again
with a low murmur, in a line of fretted foam along the sands.

There was a fascination in watching it. They felt so safe in their
eyrie in the cliff, as they looked and listened, entranced and quiet.

"I must gather some of that samphire for auntie," said Eudora,
scrambling towards the edge, where the spiky green of this sea-loving
and aromatic plant crept low on the ground in the greatest profusion.

"Wait, Dora, I will come and help you. Oh, what a funny smell it has! I
don't think I like it at all."

They were startled in their occupation by hearing footsteps above them.

"Ah, Davie, there you are!" cried Ermyn, in glee. "Come down; we are
only waiting for you to begin our singing."

"Miss Llewellyn will be here presently," he answered. "She would not
allow me to wait for her, as she was detained in the village, but I see
her now in the distance just crossing the stone wall."

"I will run and meet her," shouted Ermyn, as, springing up, she bounded
off like a young fawn.

Eudora looked wistfully after her, but it was too late to follow the
fleet runner, and she did not know whether she was pleased or not to
be left alone with David. She felt more shy of him every time they
met now, for a wall of ice seemed to have grown up between them which
puzzled them both. How they longed for the old days to come back again,
as they sat there exchanging commonplace remarks! It was a relief when
the others joined them.

"Now," said Mary, seating herself, "I am going to have a treat. You
three are going to sing me, 'Lift thine eyes to the mountains.' Please
make haste and begin."

The singers looked at each other and smiled, then the glorious melody
burst forth, rich and clear:

"Thy Keeper shall never slumber," sang Eudora, in soft contralto; while
David and Ermyn took up the words, "Never, shall never slumber," until
the last faint notes of the anthem died away in a whispered hush of
melody.

"Now, little sister," said David, when they had finished, "let us hear
you sing alone, 'Oh, rest in the Lord.'"

Ermyn obeyed.

Peace and rest sank down into each soul as the holy words fell softly
on the still evening air. Yea, hope and courage also, as more than one
heart prayed for the child-like spirit of perfect trust in a loving
Father who never slumbers, while He watches over the sons and daughters
of His care.

They talked long of this rest and peace, then of "the golden homes
beyond the sunset," and, long after the glow had faded, wended their
way homeward in the moonlight, silently, side by side.



CHAPTER XVI

LOST AND FOUND

EDWARD PRYSE had no time to linger among the exquisite beauties of
Nannie's mountain home, fraught though they were with the dearest and
most tender associations. Here he was treading in the very footsteps of
his Ethel; and where she had dwelt must henceforth be a sacred place
to him. He looked out upon the sunlit hills cradling the peaceful
lake, and thought how her dear eyes had rested on them, as in spirit
she travelled across the wide sea, yearning for one word to soothe her
aching heart. The silence and calm loveliness of nature would make her
feel yet more lonely; they could not fill the strange void, nor bear a
message to her beloved.

Sadly and mournfully Edward turned away from this beautiful and
sequestered spot, to exchange it, after a weary journey, for the tumult
and busy stir of the great port of Liverpool. When he reached it,
gaslights were blazing in the shops and thoroughfares, and enormous
buildings, telling of wealth and luxury, rose on either hand. Edward
noted nothing as he drove on, through the din and crowd, to the hotel
where he passed the night.

At an early hour the following morning, he was at the well-known
shipping agent's office. There seemed an unusual amount of business on
hand, and Edward found it no easy task to find the clerks who could be
of any assistance to him. But after patient and persistent waiting,
and being referred from one place to another, his search was not in
vain. The answers he received were gruff and unsatisfactory at first,
for the Company did not care to be reminded of one of the most serious
disasters they had ever experienced.

But Edward was firm in his determination and polite in speech, and,
quietly persisting in his inquiries, he soon received a due amount
of attention. The sheet of the "Illustrated London News" secretly
abstracted from the country inn in Scotland, and containing a picture
of the ship "Ocean Queen," was of the greatest service, for with the
date, it gave exact information concerning the disaster.

Books were referred to, and passenger lists consulted, but "Pryse" was
not among the names of the passengers who had sailed in the fatal ship.
This was what Edward had expected, as the thought had struck him on his
journey that for some reason Ethel might have assumed her maiden name.
Was there a passenger of the name of Macdonald? How his heart throbbed
in expectation of the answer! Yes, there was, in the steerage list:
"Ethel Macdonald, aged twenty-five, and infant."

There was nothing further to be gained from the hard-looking clerks,
whose information had just crushed out the last spark of hope that
Ethel might after all be somewhere in the world. What did it concern
them that a lonely stranger had timidly moved among them fourteen
years ago? How had she looked? What did she say? Who knew, and who
cared? Edward felt hopeless tears rush into his eyes as he asked
himself these questions. He was just leaving the office, his confused
thoughts surging through his brain, when he caught sight of a
respectable-looking porter, whose face he fancied he had seen when in
Liverpool before.

The man touched his cap to him, and something within him made Edward
stop and speak.

"I think I can remember your face," he said; "you must have been in the
Company's service for many years."

"Yes, sir," replied the man; "I pretty well know the look of this
place, for I have been about it twenty years."

"Indeed!" rejoined Mr. Pryse. "Then you must have seen a good deal of
the bustle of life in that time. I suppose, though, you never notice
who comes, or who goes in here?"

"Tisn't very often, sir, I have much occasion to. There's so many faces
that I get confused among them all, and couldn't tell at the end of the
year whether I had seen them or no. But sometimes things happen that
a lifetime wouldn't make one forget. Did you ever hear of the 'Ocean
Queen,' sir, one of our fine paddle-steamers that was wrecked off the
Welsh coast?"

Edward leaned against the wall he stood near, and a dizzy faintness
came over him, which greatly surprised his interlocutor. But he checked
himself sufficiently to answer in a hollow voice: "Yes. I have been
making inquiries concerning that very ship. Do you know anything about
her?"

"Why, sir, 'tis this way. I think it must be fourteen or fifteen years
ago, when one day I noticed a pretty young woman standing here with a
baby in her arms. She asked me a few questions, and I found she was
quite a stranger to Liverpool. It seemed as if she hardly knew what she
was saying or doing. Her dress was not like a lady's, but she had the
face and speech of one, and a more modest-looking young woman I never
saw in my life. She had come to inquire about her husband, who, she
said, had gone to America."

Edward grasped his arm. "For mercy's sake, tell me all you know about
her!"

The man looked wonderingly, but continued: "Well, sir, while I was
talking to her, she suddenly fell forward in a fainting-fit, but I
caught her before any harm was done. There she lay looking like death,
and I couldn't tell what to do with her, nor where to take her. A crowd
soon gathered round, prying and wondering. But I was so sorry for the
poor young thing, and didn't like her to be made a public spectacle of,
so I hailed a cab and took her right off to my missus, who is always a
wonderful hand at doing people good. She soon got better in our little
parlour. I think it was the crying of the baby that roused her to
herself.

"Our hearts ached when she told us her story. Ethel Macdonald was her
name; and oh, what a long journey she had taken to hear about her
husband. She wanted to go to America after him, but we told her that
would be no good, if she didn't know where he was. Then we asked her if
she had any relations, and she said, 'Yes, her husband's people lived
in South Wales; she would go to them.'

"One of them, I think she said, was a clergyman of the name of Pryse,
but I cannot recollect the name of the place she said he lived in. I
only know it was a country town somewhere on the coast. Her husband
had promised to take her there some day, she said, but they were all
strangers to her, so she would not write to them. My wife and I looked
at each other, and we both made up our minds that she should not be
turned out into the street, helpless and forlorn, for she looked so ill
and tired out, poor thing."

"God bless you," said Edward, when he could speak, "for your kindness;
you will have your reward. That lady was my wife."

The man started, and looked incredulous, but one glance at Edward's
blanched and haggard face was enough.

"Beg pardon, sir; who would thought it? And that is why you have
come to Liverpool, I suppose. Things do happen queer in this world
sometimes! I am just going home, sir; if you will come with me, I will
tell you the rest of the sad tale."

Edward obeyed mechanically, feeling as if in a dream.

"'Twas our doing, I fear, sir, that your good lady was put into the
'Ocean Queen,' but we thought we were performing the greatest kindness.
My wife's sister was then staying with us, and she had arranged to go
back to her home in Cork in this very ship. Her husband traded in a
small vessel between Ireland and a Welsh port; and being a native of
South Wales, he knew about everything and everybody in that part of the
country.

"After we had consulted together, we thought the best plan would be
for the poor lady to take her passage with my sister-in-law, who also
had a young baby. She took no end of a fancy to the lady's pretty
child. Eliza promised that as soon as they reached Cork, she would
put Mrs. Macdonald under her husband's care. He would be sure to know
whether there was a clergyman called Pryse living in the place the lady
mentioned; and he was such a good, kind man, he would not leave her
until he had discovered her friends, and seen her safely with them. My
wife will tell you the rest, sir," added the man, as he opened the door
of a quiet-looking dwelling in a back street.

A pale, middle-aged woman was sitting at work in the little parlour
into which Edward was ushered. The man, in a few low words,
communicated to her who the stranger was. She put her hand to her brow,
and looked earnestly at him, then her face filled with a reverent pity.

"Oh, sir!" said she, as the tears gathered in her eyes. "That was a sad
year for you and for us. My dear sister and her young baby we never saw
again from that day to this. The news stunned us when it came, 'All
hands lost!'

"I was so ill for weeks after, my husband thought that I should have
died. And the poor man in Ireland, who was looking out for his dear
wife, was in such a state I believe he lost his senses for a time. And
that lonely lady, with her sweet face and ways, and the darling little
child, all drowned! Oh, it was heart-rending!"

"I grieve to recall these sad facts to your memory," said Edward
gently, "but you will be doing me the greatest service on earth if you
will only tell me all you can."

"Well, sir," continued the woman, "I am sure, then, for her dear sake I
will. It seems as though it only happened yesterday, it is all so fresh
in my mind. She was only with us two days, but though the time was so
short, I got to love her dearly; for oh, she was such a Christian, and
it was a great comfort to talk to one who followed the dear Saviour
so closely. I shall never forget the last morning when I watched her
dress her baby, and saw it playing with a beautiful locket that hung
from a chain round the mother's neck. It was a gold one set round with
pearls—very valuable, I should think. Baby thought it a fine toy, and
liked having it between her little fat hands better than anything else,
and the mother laughed and told her it should be her own some day."

"I wonder," thought Edward, "if that locket was found by the fishermen!
If it was on the body, it must be in some one's possession. But, ah no,
the woman would surely have mentioned such a fact as that, for it would
be the chief clue to the child's identity. Besides, such a valuable
article as that would not be kept long by poor people; they would be
sure to turn it into money. Still if it had even been seen by some one,
there would be sufficient ground for claiming the child."

Edward started from his reverie, to see the woman watching him intently
with her sad eyes.

"God has directed my way to you," he said; "it must be so, for
otherwise I should never have found this last link in the wonderful
chain of evidence that has guided me so unerringly in the long search
for my lost ones."

Mr. Pryse then proceeded to make the most minute inquiries into
details of all that Ethel had said or done during her brief stay with
these Good Samaritans, and the kind woman told him all she knew. This
account, when added to the Diary so abruptly broken off, gave Edward a
connected story of the sweet, sorrowful life, since the day their hands
had parted, and the long good-bye was said.


It was late on Saturday night before he could manage to reach St.
Elvis, even by the most rapid travelling, so there were no curious
gossips standing about to watch him as he entered "The Jolly Sailors"
and engaged his rooms. He could see the churchyard from his window,
and stood watching late into the night the waving sycamores over those
two graves in the corner, until his heart felt ready to burst with its
sorrow.

And there down the quiet valley, with the sea breaking near it, stood
the cottage that sheltered his precious child—his one link with Ethel
in heaven. Would she be glad to call him father, when he clasped her in
his arms? Or would she turn away and say she did not need his love now?
This agonising doubt prevented sleep from visiting his eyelids until
daylight began to dawn through the dingy stuff curtains of his room.

Then he fell into a troubled but heavy slumber, from which he was only
awakened by the ringing of the church bells for morning service. Voices
and footsteps hurried by in the street below, and he caught snatches of
sentences—

"Dr. Pryse is not going to preach till afternoon," said one.

"It will be Mr. David, then, I suppose."

"Ay, ay; he's a fine preacher, that young man."

"Who would have thought of Widow Gwynne's son rising to be a minister!"

"He speaks plain enough, don't he?" said the first speaker.

"Ay, that he do, indeed; he don't mind calling things by their right
names, do he? Offence or no offence; and yet he's a good friend to the
poor."

The conversation grew distant, and Edward Pryse relapsed into his
reverie.


The afternoon sun was streaming into the church windows, showing
the dancing motes. Service had just begun, when Lettice noticed the
mysterious stranger who had taken such a fancy to her child come in
and seat himself near her, in one of the free seats by the font. Her
heart gave a little flutter when she saw him; she could not tell why.
What had he come back for? Who was he? Lettice felt quite ashamed to
discover her thoughts were causing her mind to wander utterly from the
service. She had not heard any of it for the last five minutes, nor
joined in a single response!

When they rose to sing, Ermyn handed a hymn-book to the sad-looking
gentleman, whom she recognised as the same who had taken her likeness.

Dr. Pryse ascended the pulpit-stairs, and in a distinct though
faltering and aged voice read as his text the story of the Prodigal
Son. Then, after a pause, continued:

   "What a sad and life-like picture we have here! A dearly loved son,
with every comfort and blessing round him, wishes to leave it all and
go away from his father's face.

   "'Give me the portion that falleth to me.'

   "What was it? Just the very portion that men of the world are craving
for nowadays—'his living;' all that they think life is worth; the
worship of the golden god with all his attendant train, 'the lust of
the flesh, the lust of the eyes, and the pride of life': this trinity
of evil was what the young man desired as his portion, and he got it.

   "Did it satisfy him? Was it a portion that filled him with good? Ah no!
Alone in a stranger-land, he sat and wept. Instead of the dainty meat
he had once fed upon, there was the swine's trough set before him. In
vain he held out his empty hands. 'No man gave unto him.'

   "Then he remembered the light and love in his father's dwelling. There,
in that home of peace and purity, no rough winds could come; no hunger,
no drought. Thrice blessed they who dwelt beneath its shelter! There
was not only food, but enough and to spare. Even the hired servants
could know no want. Alas for him, sitting out there alone in the cold,
with no eye to pity, no hand to raise up! But while he mused over his
desolation and misery, a light shone into his soul,—a light as bright
as that which lit up the prison darkness that surrounded Saint Peter.

   "'Arise!' it said. 'How many hired servants of my father's have bread
enough and to spare, and I perish with hunger! I will arise and go to
my father, and will say unto him, Father, I have sinned against heaven,
and before thee, and am no more worthy to be called thy son: make me
as one of thy hired servants.' 'If only I can be in my own fair home
again, and under the same roof as my father, he may give me the office
of a menial; I will serve him joyfully.'

   "Ah, but he did not know the father's love even then; he put a limit
to it. He was judging him according to his own poor standard. He could
not believe that from the day the erring one left the shelter of his
home the father had yearned for his return. My friends, have you ever
known what it was to lose a son? I do not mean by death. Has your
well-beloved child ever wandered away upon the mountains and never come
home?"

The aged pastor could get no further, for a sob choked his utterance,
as he leaned heavily on the pulpit cushion. But he checked himself. For
years he had struggled to speak to his people from this parable, but
could not. He must do it now.

   "Does not the love for the absent one," he continued, "grow deeper
and deeper as the years move on? Would you not give all you possessed
for that precious life? Forgiveness! Ah, that is only the glistening
